```text
10	
11	\paragraph{Results.} Under binary scoring, item difficulty (\texttt{item\_id}) accounts for 58.4\% of total variance, with the residual term contributing 23.1\%. All main facet effects are negligible ($<$1\% each): precision 0.9\%, prompt template, temperature, seed, and ordering each $<$0.5\%. The interesting structure appears in item-conditional interactions: prompt$\times$item is the largest at 5.5\%, followed by seed$\times$item (4.7\%), precision$\times$item (3.3\%), temperature$\times$item (2.9\%), and ordering$\times$item (0.8\%). This pattern indicates that methodological choices do not shift performance uniformly across items; instead, specific items are differentially sensitive to specific facets---prompt template variation produces the largest item-conditional effect.
12	
13	The resulting generalizability coefficient is $G_\text{item} = 0.936$, indicating that 200 items under multi-facet conditions produce highly reliable rankings. A D-study projection shows $G \geq 0.80$ is achievable with as few as 56 items in this multi-facet design.
14	
```

21	\paragraph{Results.} Table~\ref{tab:cross_model} presents the variance decomposition. Introducing model as a facet reshapes the structure: item difficulty drops from 58.4\% to 42.1\% [36.0--48.8\%], while model$\times$item interaction emerges as the second-largest component at 33.6\% [28.4--37.9\%]. The model main effect contributes only 0.7\%, confirming that models differ not in overall ability level but in \emph{which items} they find difficult.

```text
104	The previous sections establish that item selection and model$\times$item interaction dominate evaluation variance. A natural question follows: how many items does a given evaluation design need to achieve a target reliability? We address this through D-study projections that translate variance components into concrete item budgets.
105	
106	Table~\ref{tab:dstudy} compares four evaluation designs at the $G \geq 0.80$ threshold. The multi-facet design (432 conditions per item: 6 prompts $\times$ 6 seeds $\times$ 3 temperatures $\times$ 4 orderings) achieves $G = 0.936$ at 200 items and requires only 56 items for $G \geq 0.80$. A single-condition design (one fixed prompt, seed, ordering, and precision per item) reaches only $G = 0.559$ at 200 items and requires 631 items for the same threshold. The ratio between these budgets is [TBD-RATIO]$\times$, and this ratio remains stable across subset sizes from 50 to 200 items (CV = 1.9\%).
107	
108	\begin{table}[t]
109	\centering
110	\caption{D-study reproducibility budgets under different evaluation designs. The multi-facet design requires [TBD-RATIO]$\times$ fewer items than single-condition evaluation to reach $G \geq 0.80$.}
111	\label{tab:dstudy}
112	\small
113	\begin{tabular}{lcrrr}

_source line 1827 · 2026-05-21T21:44:54.268Z_

[Director 指令] [Patch — 页面溢出修复 + Abstract 确认]

## 1. 页面溢出（9→8页）

决策：§4.6 Robustness 的 5 个非核心检查（Estimator robustness、CI method、Facet specification、LOMO、Null calibration）移至 Appendix。主文保留 Item resampling（直接回应 R1-W1），加一句话引用 Appendix：

"Five additional robustness analyses (estimator comparison, CI method, facet specification, leave-one-model-out, and null calibration) are reported in Appendix~X; all confirm the stability of the reported variance structure."

这样既保留完整性又省约半页。确保 "six robustness analyses" 改为准确描述（如 "We conduct six robustness analyses; item resampling is detailed here, with five additional checks in Appendix~X"）。

## 2. Abstract 范围

保持 **42--49%** 不改。理由：
- 42-49% 是 cross-model MC 范围（§4.3 Table 3），这是论文的核心 finding
- 58.4% 是 single-model baseline（§4.1），不同分析层次
- 混写 "42-58%" 会误导读者以为是同一分析的范围

如果 Abstract 有空间，可补充 "up to 58% in single-model designs"，但不必要。

## 3. 其他

Henderson I 替换确认无误。等 [TBD-RATIO] 的 single-condition D-study 结果后我会再发一个 Patch。

141	\subsection{Robustness Checks}
142	
143	We conduct six robustness analyses to verify that the reported variance structures are not artifacts of specific analytical choices. \textbf{Item resampling} (exp-001b): three independent 200-item samples drawn from the full MMLU pool and evaluated under a BF16-only 5-facet design yield item difficulty variance shares of 73.2\%, 72.6\%, and 69.0\% (CV = 3.1\%) and $G_\text{item}$ values with CV = 0.5\%. The higher item share relative to the 6-facet baseline (58.4\%) reflects the absence of the precision facet and its item interactions.
144	\textbf{Estimator robustness}: REML and Henderson Method~I differ by $<$0.5 percentage points on all components. \textbf{CI method}: bootstrap and jackknife confidence intervals agree closely (width ratio 0.96--1.04 across components). \textbf{Facet specification}: treating facets as fixed vs.\ random changes $G_\text{item}$ by only 0.37 percentage points. \textbf{Leave-one-model-out (LOMO)}: all three-model subsets preserve the item + model$\times$item dominance pattern (item\_id 39--45\%, model$\times$item 32--37\%, $G_\text{item}$ 0.747--0.793). \textbf{Null calibration}: a Bernoulli simulation with matched item difficulties produces a variance ratio of 1.0008 relative to observed data, confirming that the decomposition does not introduce systematic bias on binary outcomes.
145	
146	% [PLACEHOLDER: §4.7 Extended Results — 8-model × 5-benchmark expansion]
147	
```

  "content": "\\appendix\n\\section{Additional Robustness Analyses}\n\\label{app:robustness}\n\n\\textbf{Estimator robustness.} REML and Henderson Method~I differ by $<$0.5 percentage points on all components.\n\n\\textbf{CI method.} Bootstrap and jackknife confidence intervals agree closely (width ratio 0.96--1.04 across components).\n\n\\textbf{Facet specification.} Treating facets as fixed vs.\\ random changes $G_\\text{item}$ by only 0.37 percentage points.\n\n\\textbf{Leave-one-model-out (LOMO).} All three-model subsets preserve the item + model$\\times$item dominance pattern (item\\_id 39--45\\%, model$\\times$item 32--37\\%, $G_\\text{item}$ 0.747--0.793).\n\n\\textbf{Null calibration.} A Bernoulli simulation with matched item difficulties produces a variance ratio of 1.0008 relative to observed data, confirming that the decomposition does not introduce systematic bias on binary outcomes.\n"
}

## 四、叙事关键更新

### 4.1 "dichotomy" 措辞调整
experiments.tex ~L85 有一句 FROZEN 的：
> "This dichotomy is not a continuous gradient"

这句需要改写。**不能说"dichotomy"了**——HellaSwag MC 需要 171 items（比 FF 的 GSM8K 55 items 还多），item budget 维度上 MC/FF 没有简单二分。

改写方向：
> The variance structure is format-conditional: MC benchmarks concentrate variance in items (34--38\%), while FF benchmarks distribute it more evenly across model, prompt, and item facets (12--24\% each). This structural difference, however, does not extend to item budgets: HellaSwag requires 171 items for $G \geq 0.80$, the highest of all benchmarks, demonstrating that format alone does not predict evaluation cost.

同样检查 abstract/intro/conclusion 中的 "dichotomy" 措辞，统一改为 "format-conditional variance structure"。

### 4.2 删除 G_item MC/FF 对比
论文中如有类似以下表述必须删除或重写：
- "MC benchmarks achieve G≥0.82 while FF benchmarks G≤0.36" — 这是 code bug 产物
- 任何暗示 FF 不可靠而 MC 可靠的表述

**正确表述**：G_item 在所有 benchmarks 上都在 0.86-0.90 范围内（multi-facet formula），format 不影响 G_item 本身。

### 4.3 新增 within-MMLU gradient 证据
在 Experiments 或 Discussion 中新增一段 item heterogeneity mechanism 的证据：

> To probe the mechanism behind item variance, we exploit the natural heterogeneity within MMLU's 57 subjects. Subjects with higher item difficulty dispersion (measured by $\text{std}(p_i)$ across models) exhibit higher item variance shares, with Spearman $\rho = 0.806$ ($p = 0.005$, $n = 10$ subjects). Notably, \texttt{high\_school\_mathematics}, the only MC subject with open-ended numerical items, shows item variance of just 10.4\%—below the free-form benchmark GSM8K (16.86\%)—suggesting that item heterogeneity, not format per se, drives variance allocation.

### 4.4 三层叙事结构指引
论文叙事应区分三个层次：
1. **Variance STRUCTURE is format-conditional**（强证据，可以 claim）：MC item_id 34-38% vs FF 17-24%，Cohen's d=1.73
2. **Optimization STRATEGY differs by format**（合理推断，可以 argue）：MC 优化 item pool，FF 需同时优化 prompt+model+item
3. **Item BUDGET is NOT format-conditional**（不能 claim）：HellaSwag MC 需要 171 items > GSM8K FF 55 items

确保论文不会在 level 3 上过度声称。

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\subsection{Generalizability Theory}
5	\label{sec:gtheory}
6	
7	Generalizability theory (G-theory) treats each measurement as one observation from a universe of admissible conditions defined by the crossed facets of an evaluation design \cite{shavelson1991generalizability, brennan2001generalizability}. The \emph{object of measurement} is the entity whose consistency is of interest. In our setting, test items ($p$) serve as the object of measurement, and the goal is to determine how reliably item-level scores generalize across the methodological conditions under which they are collected.
8	
9	A \emph{G-study} decomposes total observed variance into components $\sigma^2_x$ for each facet $x$ and their interactions. Each component quantifies how much variability that source contributes to the overall measurement. Large interaction components (e.g., $\sigma^2_{pM}$ for item-by-model) indicate that facets do not affect items uniformly but instead create differential difficulty profiles. We estimate variance components via Henderson Method~I, an unbiased ANOVA-based estimator for balanced designs \cite[Ch.~3]{brennan2001generalizability}, with 1,000-resample bootstrap confidence intervals \cite{li2023bootstrap}.
10	
11	A \emph{D-study} projects the generalizability coefficient under hypothetical designs with different numbers of conditions per facet:
12	\begin{equation}
13	G_\text{item} = \frac{\sigma^2_p}{\sigma^2_p + \sigma^2_\text{rel}}
14	\label{eq:gcoeff}
15	\end{equation}
16	where $\sigma^2_\text{rel}$ aggregates all item-by-facet interaction variances, each divided by the number of conditions for the corresponding facets \cite[Ch.~4]{brennan2001generalizability}. This prospective capability distinguishes G-theory from classical ANOVA: rather than only attributing variance post hoc, a D-study enables optimization of evaluation designs \emph{before} data collection \cite{shavelson1989gtheory}. Throughout this paper, we use the following notation: $p$ = item, $M$ = model, $\pi$ = numerical precision, $\delta$ = prompt template, $s$ = random seed, $\tau$ = sampling temperature, $o$ = answer ordering, and $\sigma^2_x$ = variance component for source $x$.
17	
18	\subsection{Experimental Design: MMLU}
19	\label{sec:exp001}
20	
21	We design a fully crossed factorial experiment (exp-001) that simultaneously varies seven facets of LLM evaluation on MMLU. Table~\ref{tab:design_matrix} summarizes the complete design matrix, and Figure~\ref{fig:design_diagram} illustrates the crossing structure.
22	
23	\paragraph{Facets.} Items ($p$): 200 MMLU questions stratified across 10 subjects (20 per subject). The stratified sampling ensures that variance estimates are not dominated by a single knowledge domain; the 10 subjects span STEM, humanities, social sciences, and professional disciplines. Models ($M$): Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, Gemma-2-9B-it, Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.3, and Qwen3-8B, a purposive sample from four major architecture families treated as a quasi-random facet (\S3.6). Numerical precision ($\pi$): BF16, FP16, and FP32, all evaluated with \texttt{enforce\_eager=True} for fair comparison across precision levels. Prompt template ($\delta$): six semantically equivalent paraphrases of the evaluation instruction \cite{sclar2024formatting}. Random seed ($s$): six fixed values (42, 123, 456, 789, 1024, 2048). Sampling temperature ($\tau$): three levels (0.0, 0.3, 0.7) spanning deterministic to moderately stochastic decoding. Answer ordering ($o$): four permutations of few-shot demonstration examples (for MMLU; structurally inert for other benchmarks, see \S\ref{sec:exp002}) \cite{pezeshkpour2024ordering}.
24	
25	\paragraph{Sample sizes.} The cross-model BF16 balanced subset yields 345,600 records (4 models $\times$ 200 items $\times$ 6 prompts $\times$ 6 seeds $\times$ 3 temperatures $\times$ 4 orderings). A single-model analysis (Llama) spans all three precisions, yielding 259,200 binary observations across 1,296 fully balanced conditions. The BF16 subset serves as the primary cross-model sample because BF16 is the standard inference precision for these architectures. Gemma-2 does not support FP16 due to vLLM numerical instability guards; cross-model analyses therefore use BF16 to maintain a balanced design.
26	
27	\begin{table}[t]
28	\centering
29	\caption{Fully crossed G-theory design. Exp-001 evaluates MMLU across all facet levels; exp-002 extends to four benchmarks with reduced crossing. $^\dagger$BF16 balanced subset for cross-model analysis.}
30	\label{tab:design_matrix}
31	\small
32	\begin{tabular}{llrr}
33	\toprule
34	Facet & Levels & exp-001 & exp-002 \\
35	\midrule
36	Item ($p$) & Stratified sample & 200 & 200$\times$4 \\
37	Model ($M$) & 4 architecture families & 4 & 4 \\
38	Precision ($\pi$) & BF16 / FP16 / FP32 & 3 & 1 \\
39	Prompt ($\delta$) & Paraphrases & 6 & 6 \\
40	Seed ($s$) & Fixed set & 6 & 6 \\
41	Temperature ($\tau$) & 0.0 / 0.3 / 0.7 & 3 & 2 \\
42	Ordering ($o$) & Demo permutations & 4 & 4 \\
43	\midrule
44	\multicolumn{2}{l}{Total records} & 345,600$^\dagger$ & 921,600 \\
45	\bottomrule
46	\end{tabular}
47	\end{table}
48	
49	\begin{figure}[t]
50	\centering
51	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig2_design_diagram}
52	\caption{Fully crossed G-theory design for LLM evaluation. Each observation is a binary correctness score determined by the crossing of seven facets: item ($p$), model ($M$), precision ($\pi$), prompt ($\delta$), seed ($s$), temperature ($\tau$), and ordering ($o$).}
53	\label{fig:design_diagram}
54	\end{figure}
55	
56	\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Extension}
57	\label{sec:exp002}
58	
59	To test whether the variance structure observed on MMLU generalizes across evaluation formats, exp-002 extends the design to four benchmarks: MMLU, ARC-Challenge, and HellaSwag (multiple-choice), and GSM8K (free-form generation). The design crosses 4 models $\times$ BF16 $\times$ 2 temperatures (0.0, 0.7) $\times$ 6 prompts $\times$ 6 seeds $\times$ 4 orderings $\times$ 200 items per benchmark, totaling 921,600 records. This reduced crossing fixes precision at BF16 and uses two temperature levels to concentrate statistical power on format-conditional differences. The ordering facet operates only on MMLU, where it selects among four permutations of the few-shot demonstration examples. For all other benchmarks, the prompt construction does not use the ordering parameter; ordering is structurally inert ($\sigma^2_{\text{ordering}} = 0$ by construction) and is retained solely for design balance. The two temperature levels bracket the most common evaluation configurations: deterministic ($\tau = 0.0$) and a moderate stochastic setting ($\tau = 0.7$) representative of practical deployment.
60	
61	% [PLACEHOLDER: 8 models × 5 benchmarks when expansion complete]
62	
63	\subsection{Evaluation Protocol}
64	\label{sec:protocol}
65	
66	All evaluations use generation-based scoring: models produce free-text responses from which correctness is determined by answer extraction, rather than by ranking answer options via token log-likelihood. This design choice follows \citet{hua2025flaw}, who show that log-likelihood scoring conflates model confidence with surface-level token biases. We report two metrics: binary correctness (primary, 1/0 per item) and text-exact-match (secondary). Binary correctness accepts any response containing the correct answer letter or numerical value after standard normalization; text-exact-match requires the generated string to match the reference answer character-for-character.
67	
68	Inference runs on vLLM~0.9.2 with default settings and without batch-invariant kernels. This configuration reflects standard practitioner conditions and preserves CUDA-level nondeterminism from floating-point non-associativity as a measurable variance source \cite{messina2026background, thinkingmachines2025batch}. Few-shot prompting follows standard protocols: 5-shot for MMLU and ARC-Challenge, 8-shot for GSM8K, and 10-shot for HellaSwag.
69	
70	\subsection{Binary Data Assumptions}
71	\label{sec:binary}
72	
73	Standard G-theory assumes continuous, approximately normal outcome data \cite{shavelson1991generalizability}. Our binary correctness scores (0/1) violate this assumption. We establish robustness through four complementary analyses.
74	
75	\paragraph{Simulation.} We generate continuous latent scores from a probit model matched to our 1,296-condition design, then dichotomize at the empirical accuracy threshold. Spearman rank correlation between binary and continuous variance component rankings is $\rho = 0.732$ (range 0.716--0.751 across accuracy levels $p \in \{0.5, 0.6, 0.7, 0.8\}$), confirming that Henderson Method~I recovers the relative ordering of variance sources despite binary response data. Henderson Method~I identifies \texttt{item\_id} as the dominant variance source in all 100 simulation replications.
76	
77	\paragraph{GLMM complement.} A generalized linear mixed model (GLMM) with marginal logit link produces variance component rankings that perfectly preserve all seven grouped component orderings ($\rho = 1.0$) relative to Henderson Method~I \cite{jiang2018bayesian, tuerlinckx2006glmm}.
78	
79	\paragraph{Accuracy-range stability.} The \texttt{item\_id} variance share remains stable across subsets with mean accuracy from 0.62 to 0.72 ($r = -0.048$). This stability rules out distortion from binary floor or ceiling effects at observed accuracy levels.
80	
81	\paragraph{Estimator guarantee.} Henderson Method~I produces unbiased variance component estimates for balanced designs regardless of the outcome distribution \cite[Ch.~12]{brennan2001generalizability}. Our fully crossed design satisfies this balance condition.
82	
83	\subsection{Model-as-Facet Justification}
84	\label{sec:model_facet}
85	
86	Treating four purposively selected models as a quasi-random facet requires empirical validation. We provide three forms of evidence.
87	
88	D-study cross-validation: variance components estimated from all $\binom{4}{3} = 4$ three-model subsets predict the four-model generalizability coefficient. The predicted $G = 0.816$ for $n_\text{model} = 4$ closely matches the observed $G = 0.820$ ($\Delta = 0.004$). This agreement confirms that the model facet behaves as expected under random-effects assumptions.
89	
90	Leave-one-model-out (LOMO) stability: all four three-model subsets preserve \texttt{item\_id} as the dominant component and rank \texttt{model$\times$item} as the second-largest ($G_\text{item}$ range: 0.747--0.793). The consistency of rankings across all subsets indicates that no single model disproportionately drives the observed variance structure.
91	
92	Forward projection: D-study estimates predict $G = 0.893$ at eight models. This result supports the quasi-random facet assumption for the broader population of instruction-tuned 7--9B architectures.
93	
94	% [PLACEHOLDER: 8-model expansion strengthens to ~0.893 observed]
95

Exp-001 evaluates MMLU across all facet levels; exp-002 extends to four benchmarks with reduced crossing. $^\\dagger$BF16 balanced subset for cross-model analysis.}\n\\label{tab:design_matrix}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llrr}\n\\toprule\nFacet & Levels & exp-001 & exp-002 \\\\\n\\midrule\nItem ($p$) & Stratified sample & 200 & 200$\\times$4 \\\\\nModel ($M$) & 4 architecture families & 4 & 4 \\\\\nPrecision ($\\pi$) & BF16 / FP16 / FP32 & 3 & 1 \\\\\nPrompt ($\\delta$) & Paraphrases & 6 & 6 \\\\\nSeed ($s$) & Fixed set & 6 & 6 \\\\\nTemperature ($\\tau$) & 0.0 / 0.3 / 0.7 & 3 & 2 \\\\\nOrdering ($o$) & Demo permutations & 4 & 4 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{l}{Total records} & 345,600$^\\dagger$ & 921,600 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig2_design_diagram}\n\\caption{Fully crossed G-theory design for LLM evaluation. Each observation is a binary correctness score determined by the crossing of seven facets: item ($p$), model ($M$), precision ($\\pi$), prompt ($\\delta$), seed ($s$), temperature ($\\tau$), and ordering ($o$).}\n\\label{fig:design_diagram}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Extension}\n\\label{sec:exp002}\n\nTo test whether the variance structure observed on MMLU generalizes across evaluation formats, exp-002 extends the design to four benchmarks: MMLU, ARC-Challenge, and HellaSwag (multiple-choice), and GSM8K (free-form generation). The design crosses 4 models $\\times$ BF16 $\\times$ 2 temperatures (0.0, 0.7) $\\times$ 6 prompts $\\times$ 6 seeds $\\times$ 4 orderings $\\times$ 200 items per benchmark, totaling 921,600 records. This reduced crossing fixes precision at BF16 and uses two temperature levels to concentrate statistical power on format-conditional differences. The ordering facet operates only on MMLU, where it selects among four permutations of the few-shot demonstration examples. For all other benchmarks, the prompt construction does not use the ordering parameter; ordering is structurally inert ($\\sigma^2_{\\text{ordering}} = 0$ by construction) and is retained solely for design balance. The two temperature levels bracket the most common evaluation configurations: deterministic ($\\tau = 0.0$) and a moderate stochastic setting ($\\tau = 0.7$) representative of practical deployment.\n\n% [PLACEHOLDER: 8 models × 5 benchmarks when expansion complete]\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation Protocol}\n\\label{sec:protocol}\n\nAll evaluations use generation-based scoring: models produce free-text responses from which correctness is determined by answer extraction, rather than by ranking answer options via token log-likelihood. This design choice follows \\citet{hua2025flaw}, who show that log-likelihood scoring conflates model confidence with surface-level token biases. We report two metrics: binary correctness (primary, 1/0 per item) and text-exact-match (secondary). Binary correctness accepts any response containing the correct answer letter or numerical value after standard normalization; text-exact-match requires the generated string to match the reference answer character-for-character.\n\nInference runs on vLLM~0.9.2 with default settings and without batch-invariant kernels. This configuration reflects standard practitioner conditions and preserves CUDA-level nondeterminism from floating-point non-associativity as a measurable variance source \\cite{messina2026background, thinkingmachines2025batch}. Few-shot prompting follows standard protocols: 5-shot for MMLU and ARC-Challenge, 8-shot for GSM8K, and 10-shot for HellaSwag.\n\n\\subsection{Binary Data Assumptions}\n\\label{sec:binary}\n\nStandard G-theory assumes continuous, approximately normal outcome data \\cite{shavelson1991generalizability}. Our binary correctness scores (0/1) violate this assumption. We establish robustness through four complementary analyses.\n\n\\paragraph{Simulation.} We generate continuous latent scores from a probit model matched to our 1,296-condition design, then dichotomize at the empirical accuracy threshold. Spearman rank correlation between binary and continuous variance component rankings is $\\rho = 0.732$ (range 0.716--0.751 across accuracy levels $p \\in \\{0.5, 0.6, 0.7, 0.8\\}$), confirming that Henderson Method~I recovers the relative ordering of variance sources despite binary response data. Henderson Method~I identifies \\texttt{item\\_id} as the dominant variance source in all 100 simulation replications.\n\n\\paragraph{GLMM complement.} A generalized linear mixed model (GLMM) with marginal logit link produces variance component rankings that perfectly preserve all seven grouped component orderings ($\\rho = 1.0$) relative to Henderson Method~I \\cite{jiang2018bayesian, tuerlinckx2006glmm}.\n\n\\paragraph{Accuracy-range stability.} The \\texttt{item\\_id} variance share remains stable across subsets with mean accuracy from 0.62 to 0.72 ($r = -0.048$). This stability rules out distortion from binary floor or ceiling effects at observed accuracy levels.\n\n\\paragraph{Estimator guarantee.} Henderson Method~I produces unbiased variance component estimates for balanced designs regardless of the outcome distribution \\cite[Ch.~12]{brennan2001generalizability}. Our fully crossed design satisfies this balance condition.\n\n\\subsection{Model-as-Facet Justification}\n\\label{sec:model_facet}\n\nTreating four purposively selected models as a quasi-random facet requires empirical validation. We provide three forms of evidence.\n\nD-study cross-validation: variance components estimated from all $\\binom{4}{3} = 4$ three-model subsets predict the four-model generalizability coefficient. The predicted $G = 0.816$ for $n_\\text{model} = 4$ closely matches the observed $G = 0.820$ ($\\Delta = 0.004$). This agreement confirms that the model facet behaves as expected under random-effects assumptions.\n\nLeave-one-model-out (LOMO) stability: all four three-model subsets preserve \\texttt{item\\_id} as the dominant component and rank \\texttt{model$\\times$item} as the second-largest ($G_\\text{item}$ range: 0.747--0.793). The consistency of rankings across all subsets indicates that no single model disproportionately drives the observed variance structure.\n\nForward projection: D-study estimates predict $G = 0.893$ at eight models. This result supports the quasi-random facet assumption for the broader population of instruction-tuned 7--9B architectures.\n\n% [PLACEHOLDER: 8-model expansion strengthens to ~0.893 observed]\n",

      "totalLines": 95

```text
1	Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation results vary across reproductions, but variance sources interact, and their relative importance shifts with the benchmark format. No unified factorial framework exists to decompose these sources simultaneously across benchmarks. We apply Generalizability theory (G-theory) in a crossed design with seven facets across four models and four benchmarks, totaling 1.27 million evaluation records. The variance structure is format-conditional: on multiple-choice benchmarks, item selection accounts for 42--49\% of total variance, while on the free-form benchmark GSM8K, model and prompt template each contribute 12--15\% with no single dominant source. Model$\times$item interaction (33.6\%) is the hidden second-largest component, larger than all methodology main effects combined. Under binary scoring, BF16 and FP16 numerical precisions are functionally interchangeable ($<$0.1\% interaction). Multi-facet designs require [TBD-RATIO]$\times$ fewer items than single-condition designs to reach a generalizability coefficient of 0.80 (56 versus 631 items). We release an open-source G-theory pipeline and Decision study (D-study) budget calculator for evaluation design optimization.
```

_source line 1894 · 2026-05-22T00:03:57.243Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01DTbZWRZmd5HaAz1qAh7zzu`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	On multiple-choice benchmarks, item selection drives 42--49\% of evaluation variance while prompt wording is negligible ($<$1\%). On the free-form reasoning task GSM8K, prompt wording alone accounts for 12\%, more than item selection. These numbers, drawn from our factorial analysis of 1.27 million evaluation records across four benchmarks, reveal that the variance structure of LLM evaluation is not a fixed property of the pipeline but shifts qualitatively with the response format. Current evaluation practice treats variance sources as independent nuisance factors and controls them one at a time: one study perturbs prompts, another varies precision, a third changes item subsets. This piecemeal approach cannot capture interactions between sources, nor can it predict how the relative importance of each source changes across evaluation settings. Leaderboard rankings built on single-condition evaluations are therefore sensitive to methodological choices that remain unreported and uncontrolled \citep{rodriguez2021leaderboards, dehghani2021benchmark}. No unified factorial framework exists to decompose these sources simultaneously, quantify their interactions, and compare the resulting variance structure across benchmarks.
5	
6	Existing studies have examined individual variance sources in isolation, each implicitly assuming separability. \citet{sclar2024formatting} documented up to 76\% accuracy swings from prompt formatting perturbations but held hardware and item selection fixed. BrittleBench \citep{brittlebench2026} found 50\% variance from prompt perturbation under the same constraint. \citet{yuan2025layercast} showed that numerical precision and GPU type cause up to 9\% accuracy variation but provided no factorial attribution to specific sources or their interactions. \citet{mesting2026hidden} applied Generalizability theory (G-theory) to LLM evaluation and decomposed variance across five methodology facets on a single benchmark; judge identity dominated at 43.8\%, but the study included no cross-benchmark comparison and excluded infrastructure facets such as numerical precision. Studies of answer ordering \citep{pezeshkpour2024ordering} and hyperparameter sensitivity \citep{dodge2020finetuning, henderson2018deep} have documented additional sources of variation, again in isolation from the factors above. Item Response Theory (IRT) approaches \citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks, lalor2019irt} model item difficulty parameters to construct efficient benchmarks but do not account for prompt, seed, or ordering facets. \citet{bouthillier2021accounting} established ANOVA-based variance decomposition as a principled template for ML reproducibility, but this work predates the LLM evaluation context where benchmark format introduces an additional structural variable. Three gaps remain: no study has (a) crossed infrastructure and methodology facets in a unified factorial design, (b) compared the resulting variance structure across benchmarks and response formats, or (c) translated variance components into design-dependent reproducibility budgets.
7	
8	We address these gaps with a multi-benchmark G-theory study \citep{shavelson1991generalizability, brennan2001generalizability} that crosses seven facets (item, model, numerical precision, prompt template, random seed, sampling temperature, and answer ordering) across four models and four benchmarks (MMLU, ARC-Challenge, HellaSwag, and GSM8K), totaling 1.27 million records. The central finding is that variance structure is format-conditional. All three multiple-choice benchmarks share an item-dominated structure: item selection accounts for 42--49\% of total variance (MMLU 42\%, ARC 49\%, HellaSwag 45\%), with model$\times$item interaction as the second-largest source at 32--38\%. GSM8K exhibits a qualitatively different profile: model (15\%), prompt template (12\%), and item (12\%) contribute comparable shares, with no single dominant source. This is a dichotomy, not a gradient. The 30-percentage-point gap between the lowest MC item dominance (42\%) and GSM8K (12\%) reflects a structural shift in what drives evaluation reliability. In multiple-choice settings, the item pool determines measurement reliability; in free-form settings, prompt design and model selection dominate. A second finding is that model$\times$item interaction (33.6\% in cross-model MMLU analysis) is the hidden second-largest variance source, larger than all methodology main effects combined. Models differ not in overall ability but in which specific items they find difficult. D-study projections translate these findings into practical design guidance: the format-conditional structure implies that reliability optimization strategies must differ between MC and free-form benchmarks. Figure~\ref{fig:cross_benchmark} visualizes the contrast across all four benchmarks.
9	
10	Our contributions are as follows:
11	\begin{enumerate}
12	    \item \textbf{Format-conditional variance structure.} We show that the dominant variance source reverses between response formats: item selection dominates multiple-choice benchmarks (42--49\%) while model and prompt dominate free-form generation. Model$\times$item interaction (33.6\%) is the hidden second-largest source in cross-model analysis. D-study cross-validation confirms the structure (predicted $G = 0.816$, observed $G = 0.820$).
13	
14	    \item \textbf{Precision separability.} Under binary scoring, BF16 and FP16 yield functionally identical results ($<$0.1\% interaction with all other facets). This separability is metric-conditional: text-exact-match scoring reveals a 1.3\% precision$\times$temperature interaction absent under binary scoring.
15	
16	    \item \textbf{Design-dependent reproducibility budgets.} Multi-facet evaluation designs require [TBD-RATIO]$\times$ fewer items than single-condition designs to achieve $G \geq 0.80$ (56 versus 631 items). IRT-selected 116-item subsets yield $G = 0.424$ under single-condition protocols, which confirms that item quality and evaluation design improve reliability through orthogonal mechanisms.
17	
18	    \item \textbf{Open-source G-theory pipeline and D-study calculator.} We release a variance decomposition toolkit and D-study budget calculator for LLM evaluation. Practitioners can compute the number of items, models, and conditions needed for a target reliability before data collection.
19	\end{enumerate}
```

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	This study establishes that the variance structure of LLM evaluation is format-conditional. Across three multiple-choice benchmarks, item selection accounts for 42--49\% of total variance, while on the free-form benchmark GSM8K, model (15\%), prompt (12\%), and item (12\%) contribute comparable shares with no single dominant source. This is a qualitative dichotomy, not a gradient. Model$\times$item interaction (33.6\%) is the hidden second-largest source: models differ in which items they find difficult, not in overall ability. Our cross-benchmark D-study framework extends the single-benchmark G-theory approach of \citet{mesting2026hidden} to format-conditional comparison and actionable design budgets.
5	
6	The format-conditional structure implies distinct optimization strategies. For multiple-choice benchmarks, item pool composition is the primary reliability lever; varying prompts or seeds contributes negligible variance. For free-form benchmarks, prompt design and model selection matter as much as item selection, and evaluation protocols should systematically vary both. Across formats, multi-facet designs are [TBD-RATIO]$\times$ more efficient than single-condition designs (56 versus 631 items for $G \geq 0.80$). BF16 and FP16 are interchangeable under binary scoring on single-GPU inference, which eliminates precision as a required control variable. The released D-study calculator enables practitioners to determine the number of items, models, and conditions needed for a target reliability before data collection.
7	
8	Three extensions are planned. An 8-model expansion will strengthen the quasi-random model facet assumption by sampling a broader population of instruction-tuned architectures. Multi-GPU tensor parallelism will test whether precision separability holds under distributed numerical non-associativity. Fine-tuning variance analysis will quantify how adaptation reshapes the item difficulty profiles that drive model$\times$item interaction.
```

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related}
3	
4	\paragraph{Variance Decomposition in ML/NLP Evaluation.}
5	Factorial decomposition of evaluation variance has roots in the ML reproducibility literature. \citet{bouthillier2021accounting} introduced an ANOVA-based template that decomposes benchmark variance across implementation details, hyperparameters, and data splits, establishing that design choices account for a substantial share of reproducibility failures. Several subsequent studies applied similar decomposition logic to specific facets of model evaluation \citep{dodge2020finetuning, henderson2018deep, magnusson2023reproducibility}. \citet{mesting2026hidden} brought Generalizability theory into LLM evaluation, decomposing variance across five methodology facets on a single benchmark; judge identity dominated at 43.8\%, with prompt$\times$judge interaction at 12.4\%. However, this study used a single benchmark, included no infrastructure facets such as numerical precision, and fixed the judge as an LLM evaluator rather than isolating other sources. \citet{kunievsky2025intent} proposed an alternative decomposition that separates intent uncertainty, articulation variance, and model uncertainty, but this framework does not cross infrastructure and methodology facets in a factorial design. Our work extends these approaches by crossing seven facets across four benchmarks, enabling direct comparison of variance structures across evaluation formats.
6	
7	\paragraph{Infrastructure and Prompt Variance.}
8	A parallel line of work has examined individual variance sources in isolation. On the infrastructure side, \citet{yuan2025layercast} documented up to 9\% accuracy variation from numerical precision and GPU type but provided no factorial attribution. \citet{messina2026background} formalized the concept of background temperature from floating-point non-associativity in GPU computation, and \citet{thinkingmachines2025batch} proposed batch-invariant kernels to mitigate CUDA nondeterminism. On the methodology side, \citet{sclar2024formatting} found up to 76\% accuracy swings from prompt formatting changes on fixed hardware, and BrittleBench \citep{brittlebench2026} reported 50\% variance from prompt perturbation. \citet{pezeshkpour2024ordering} showed that answer ordering in multiple-choice benchmarks introduces systematic bias, while \citet{bui2025seeds} and \citet{biderman2024lessons} documented sensitivity to random seeds and training configurations. Each of these studies isolates one factor while holding others fixed, which precludes estimating interactions between sources or comparing their relative magnitudes within a unified design.
9	
10	\paragraph{IRT and Efficient Benchmarking.}
11	Item Response Theory (IRT) offers a complementary perspective on evaluation design by modeling item difficulty and discrimination parameters. \citet{lalor2019irt} introduced IRT-based item analysis for NLP benchmarks, and \citet{rodriguez2021leaderboards} showed that IRT reveals hidden properties of evaluation items that raw accuracy scores obscure. Building on this foundation, \citet{polo2024tinybenchmarks} developed tinyBenchmarks, using IRT-based subsampling to reduce evaluation cost while preserving ranking accuracy, and \citet{zhou2026irt} recommended a 116-item subset via PSN-IRT for efficient MMLU evaluation. \citet{perlitz2023efficient} explored related strategies for benchmark compression. These approaches optimize item selection for latent trait estimation but do not model variance from prompt, seed, or ordering facets. IRT-selected items evaluated under a single methodological condition can therefore systematically underestimate the number of items required for reliable measurement across diverse evaluation settings.
12	
13	\paragraph{Binary Data and Psychometrics Foundations.}
14	Our application of G-theory to binary correctness scores draws on established psychometric methodology. \citet{shavelson1991generalizability} and \citet{brennan2001generalizability} provide the canonical framework for multi-facet reliability analysis. \citet{mushquash2006gtheory} extended G-theory to binary data, and \citet{jiang2018bayesian} developed logistic-link generalized G-theory for binary outcomes. \citet{tuerlinckx2006glmm} established the connection between generalized linear mixed models and G-theory variance components. These methods ground our use of Henderson Method~I for binary correctness scores (\S\ref{sec:binary}).
15	
16	\paragraph{Positioning.}
17	Relative to \citet{mesting2026hidden}, we fix the judge (generation-based scoring) to isolate the remaining facets; their judge-dominated variance (43.8\%) would otherwise mask all other sources. Our multi-benchmark design reveals format-conditional structure that single-benchmark studies cannot detect. Relative to IRT approaches \citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks, zhou2026irt}, G-theory and IRT optimize orthogonal dimensions of evaluation design: IRT selects informative items, while G-theory determines the conditions needed for a target reliability. Our D-study quantifies this complementarity: multi-facet designs require [TBD-RATIO]$\times$ fewer items than single-condition protocols for equivalent reliability. Relative to isolated-factor studies \citep{yuan2025layercast, sclar2024formatting, brittlebench2026}, our design crosses infrastructure and methodology facets within a single factorial framework, enabling interaction estimation that per-factor studies cannot provide.
```

</details>

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01RXve6GaPCjfUyeT5hEvJu7`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	
3	We apply the G-theory framework from Section~3 to a fully crossed evaluation design spanning multiple models, benchmarks, and methodological facets. All experiments use open-weight 7--9B parameter models evaluated with vLLM~0.9.2 on NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 GPUs (96GB). Binary correctness (1/0) serves as the primary outcome measure throughout; generation-based scoring avoids the log-likelihood artifacts identified by \citet{hua2025flaw}.
4	
5	\subsection{Single-Model Baseline}
6	
7	We first establish the variance structure within a single model (Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct) on MMLU to isolate methodological facet contributions before introducing cross-model variation.
8	
9	\paragraph{Design.} The G-study crosses six facets: 200 items $\times$ 3 numerical precisions (BF16, FP16, FP32) $\times$ 6 prompt templates $\times$ 6 random seeds $\times$ 3 temperature levels (0.0, 0.3, 0.7) $\times$ 4 answer orderings, yielding 259,200 binary observations. Variance components are estimated via Henderson Method~I with bootstrap confidence intervals (1,000 resamples).
10	
11	\paragraph{Results.} Under binary scoring, item difficulty (\texttt{item\_id}) accounts for 58.4\% of total variance, with the residual term contributing 23.1\%. All main facet effects are negligible ($<$1\% each): precision 0.9\%, prompt template, temperature, seed, and ordering each $<$0.5\%. The interesting structure appears in item-conditional interactions: prompt$\times$item is the largest at 5.5\%, followed by seed$\times$item (4.7\%), precision$\times$item (3.3\%), temperature$\times$item (2.9\%), and ordering$\times$item (0.8\%). This pattern indicates that methodological choices do not shift performance uniformly across items; instead, specific items are differentially sensitive to specific facets---prompt template variation produces the largest item-conditional effect.
12	
13	The resulting generalizability coefficient is $G_\text{item} = 0.936$, indicating that 200 items under multi-facet conditions produce highly reliable rankings. A D-study projection shows $G \geq 0.80$ is achievable with as few as 56 items in this multi-facet design.
14	
15	For text-exact-match scoring (requiring verbatim string matches), the variance structure shifts dramatically: temperature dominates at 79.7\% of variance, and $G_\text{item}$ collapses to 0.007. This metric-conditional divergence motivates our exclusive use of binary correctness for the remaining analyses.
16	
17	\subsection{Cross-Model Variance}
18	
19	We extend the single-model design to four models: Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, Gemma-2-9B-it, Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.3, and Qwen3-8B. A balanced BF16 subset (345,600 records) serves as the primary analysis sample, chosen because BF16 is the standard inference precision for these architectures.
20	
21	\paragraph{Results.} Table~\ref{tab:cross_model} presents the variance decomposition. Introducing model as a facet reshapes the structure: item difficulty drops from 58.4\% to 42.1\% [36.0--48.8\%], while model$\times$item interaction emerges as the second-largest component at 33.6\% [28.4--37.9\%]. The model main effect contributes only 0.7\%, confirming that models differ not in overall ability level but in \emph{which items} they find difficult.
22	
23	\begin{table}[t]
24	\centering
25	\caption{Variance decomposition for the cross-model MMLU design (4 models, BF16, 345,600 records). Components estimated via Henderson Method~I with 1,000-resample bootstrap CIs. $G_\text{item} = 0.820$ [0.782--0.861].}
26	\label{tab:cross_model}
27	\small
28	\begin{tabular}{lrl}
29	\toprule
30	Component & Var.\% & 95\% CI \\
31	\midrule
32	\textbf{item\_id} & \textbf{42.1} & [36.0--48.8] \\
33	\underline{model$\times$item} & \underline{33.6} & [28.4--37.9] \\
34	residual & 19.5 & [17.2--21.8] \\
35	precision$\times$item & 1.3 & [0.8--1.9] \\
36	model & 0.7 & [0.2--1.5] \\
37	prompt$\times$item & 0.6 & [0.3--1.0] \\
38	temperature$\times$item & 0.6 & [0.3--1.0] \\
39	seed$\times$item & 0.4 & [0.1--0.8] \\
40	ordering$\times$item & 0.3 & [0.1--0.6] \\
41	all others & $<$0.1 & \\
42	\bottomrule
43	\end{tabular}
44	\end{table}
45	
46	The per-model decomposition of interaction magnitude reveals meaningful asymmetry: Mistral concentrates 29.3\% of the total model$\times$item variance, followed by Qwen (27.1\%), Gemma (24.0\%), and Llama (19.7\%). This ordering suggests that instruction-tuning strategies and pretraining data composition produce model-specific item difficulty profiles.
47	
48	\paragraph{D-study validation.} To test whether G-theory projections generalize beyond the training sample, we estimate a D-study on 3-model subsets and predict the 4-model coefficient. The predicted $G = 0.816$ for $n_\text{model} = 4$ closely matches the observed $G = 0.820$ ($\Delta = 0.004$). Forward projections indicate $G = 0.848$ at 5 models, $G = 0.893$ at 8 models, and $G \geq 0.90$ requires approximately 10 models.
49	
50	\subsection{Format-Conditional Variance Structure}
51	\label{sec:format_conditional}
52	
53	The preceding analyses use only MMLU, a multiple-choice benchmark. We now test whether the item-dominated variance structure generalizes across evaluation formats by extending to four benchmarks spanning two response formats.
54	
55	\paragraph{Design.} The expanded design (exp-002) crosses 4 models $\times$ 4 benchmarks $\times$ BF16 $\times$ 2 temperatures (0.0, 0.7) $\times$ 6 prompts $\times$ 6 seeds $\times$ 4 orderings $\times$ 200 items per benchmark, totaling 921,600 records. The benchmarks include three multiple-choice tasks (MMLU, ARC-Challenge, HellaSwag) and one free-form generation task (GSM8K). The ordering facet operates only on MMLU, where it selects among four permutations of the few-shot demonstration examples. For all other benchmarks, the prompt construction does not use the ordering parameter; ordering is structurally inert ($\sigma^2_{\text{ordering}} = 0$ by construction) and is retained solely for design balance.
56	
57	% [PLACEHOLDER: expand to 8 models × 5 benchmarks (+ MATH) when data ready]
58	
59	\paragraph{Results.} Table~\ref{tab:cross_benchmark} reveals a qualitative structural shift between formats. For all three multiple-choice benchmarks, item difficulty is the dominant component (MMLU 42\%, ARC 49\%, HellaSwag 45\%), and model$\times$item interaction is the second largest (32--38\%). This structure is consistent with the cross-model analysis in Section~4.2.
60	
61	GSM8K exhibits a fundamentally different variance profile. Item difficulty accounts for only 12\% of variance. Instead, model (15\%), prompt template (12\%), and model$\times$prompt interaction (9\%) become the leading components. The residual term absorbs 35\% of variance, indicating substantial unexplained variation from seed and temperature effects in free-form generation.
62	
63	\begin{table}[t]
64	\centering
65	\caption{Format-conditional variance structure across four benchmarks (exp-002, 921,600 records). Top-5 components plus residual per benchmark. MC = multiple-choice, FF = free-form. Bootstrap confidence intervals are reported in the supplementary material.}
66	\label{tab:cross_benchmark}
67	\small
68	\begin{tabular}{l rrrr}
69	\toprule
70	 & \multicolumn{3}{c}{MC} & FF \\
71	\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \cmidrule(lr){5-5}
72	Component & MMLU & ARC & Hella. & GSM8K \\
73	\midrule
74	\textbf{item\_id} & \textbf{42} & \textbf{49} & \textbf{45} & 12 \\
75	model$\times$item & 34 & 32 & 38 & 8 \\
76	\textbf{model} & 1 & 1 & 1 & \textbf{15} \\
77	prompt & $<$1 & $<$1 & $<$1 & 12 \\
78	model$\times$prompt & $<$1 & $<$1 & $<$1 & 9 \\
79	residual & 19 & 15 & 13 & 35 \\
80	\bottomrule
81	\end{tabular}
82	% [PLACEHOLDER: add MATH column + 8-model rows when data ready]
83	\end{table}
84	
85	This dichotomy is not a continuous gradient. The gap between the lowest MC item dominance (MMLU, 42\%) and GSM8K (12\%) represents a qualitative shift in what drives evaluation variance. In multiple-choice settings, the item pool determines reliability; in free-form settings, prompt design and model selection dominate. Figure~\ref{fig:cross_benchmark} visualizes this structural contrast.
86	
87	\begin{figure}[t]
88	\centering
89	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig1_cross_benchmark}
90	\caption{Variance decomposition by benchmark format. Multiple-choice benchmarks (MMLU, ARC, HellaSwag) share an item-dominated structure, while free-form GSM8K distributes variance across model, prompt, and interaction components.}
91	\label{fig:cross_benchmark}
92	\end{figure}
93	
94	\subsection{Precision Separability}
95	
96	Prior work has identified numerical precision as a potential source of evaluation variance \cite{yuan2025layercast}. Our multi-facet design allows us to isolate the precision contribution while controlling for all other facets simultaneously.
97	
98	Under binary scoring with BF16 and FP16, the mean accuracy difference is 0.23 percentage points. After excluding FP32, the precision main effect drops to 0.0006\% of total variance. The FP32 anomaly traces to a vLLM implementation detail (\texttt{enforce\_eager} mode required for FP32 inference), not a fundamental precision effect.
99	
100	Precision does not interact with other methodological facets under binary scoring: all precision$\times$methodology interactions contribute $<$0.1\% of variance. Under text-exact-match scoring, however, a precision$\times$temperature interaction emerges at 1.3\%, consistent with the metric-conditional pattern observed in Section~4.1. The practical implication is clear: for standard binary evaluation at BF16/FP16, numerical precision is a negligible variance source that need not be controlled. This finding is specific to single-GPU inference and does not address multi-GPU tensor parallelism, where numerical non-associativity introduces additional variance sources \cite{shanmugavelu2024floating}.
101	
102	\subsection{Design-Dependent Reproducibility Budgets}
103	
104	The previous sections establish that item selection and model$\times$item interaction dominate evaluation variance. A natural question follows: how many items does a given evaluation design need to achieve a target reliability? We address this through D-study projections that translate variance components into concrete item budgets.
105	
106	Table~\ref{tab:dstudy} compares four evaluation designs at the $G \geq 0.80$ threshold. The multi-facet design (432 conditions per item: 6 prompts $\times$ 6 seeds $\times$ 3 temperatures $\times$ 4 orderings) achieves $G = 0.936$ at 200 items and requires only 56 items for $G \geq 0.80$. A single-condition design (one fixed prompt, seed, ordering, and precision per item) reaches only $G = 0.559$ at 200 items and requires 631 items for the same threshold. The ratio between these budgets is [TBD-RATIO]$\times$, and this ratio remains stable across subset sizes from 50 to 200 items (CV = 1.9\%).
107	
108	\begin{table}[t]
109	\centering
110	\caption{D-study reproducibility budgets under different evaluation designs. The multi-facet design requires [TBD-RATIO]$\times$ fewer items than single-condition evaluation to reach $G \geq 0.80$.}
111	\label{tab:dstudy}
112	\small
113	\begin{tabular}{lcrrr}
114	\toprule
115	Design & Cond./item & $n_{G \geq 0.80}$ & $G_{200}$ & $G_{116}$ \\
116	\midrule
117	Multi-facet & 432 & \textbf{56} & \textbf{0.936} & \textbf{0.882} \\
118	% PENDING: Partial, Single-condition, IRT rows await Henderson I recalculation
119	Partial (4 cond.) & 4 & 189 & 0.810 & 0.721 \\
120	Single-condition & 1 & 631 & 0.559 & 0.424 \\
121	\midrule
122	IRT baseline & \multicolumn{1}{c}{---} & 116 & --- & 0.424$^\dagger$ \\
123	\bottomrule
124	\end{tabular}
125	
126	\vspace{2pt}
127	{\scriptsize $^\dagger$\,PSN-IRT item count of 116 \cite{zhou2026irt} evaluated under single-condition protocol yields $G = 0.424$. See text for interpretation.}
128	\end{table}
129	
130	The comparison with IRT-based efficient benchmarking is instructive. \citet{zhou2026irt} use IRT to select 116 informative items from MMLU, optimizing for item discrimination. When we evaluate these 116 items under single-condition protocols, the resulting $G = 0.424$ falls well below the reliability threshold. This is not a failure of IRT item selection; rather, it reflects that IRT optimizes a different objective (latent trait estimation) that does not account for methodological facet variance. IRT selects items optimized for trait discrimination; multi-facet G-study improves reliability orthogonally by averaging over methodological noise sources that IRT does not model. Under the multi-facet design, 116 items yield $G = 0.882$, confirming that the reliability gap is driven by evaluation design, not item quality.
131	
132	Figure~\ref{fig:dstudy} plots the D-study projection curves for each design. The multi-facet curve saturates rapidly (diminishing returns beyond $\sim$150 items), while the single-condition curve rises slowly and does not reach $G = 0.80$ within typical benchmark sizes.
133	
134	\begin{figure}[t]
135	\centering
136	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig3_dstudy_curves}
137	\caption{D-study projection curves for three evaluation designs. The multi-facet design ([TBD-RATIO]$\times$ fewer items) reaches $G \geq 0.80$ at 56 items; the single-condition design requires 631 items. The shaded band shows 95\% bootstrap CIs.}
138	\label{fig:dstudy}
139	\end{figure}
140	
141	\subsection{Robustness Checks}
142	
143	We conduct six robustness analyses to verify that the reported variance structures are not artifacts of specific analytical choices. \textbf{Item resampling} (exp-001b): three independent 200-item samples drawn from the full MMLU pool and evaluated under a BF16-only 5-facet design yield item difficulty variance shares of 73.2\%, 72.6\%, and 69.0\% (CV = 3.1\%) and $G_\text{item}$ values with CV = 0.5\%. The higher item share relative to the 6-facet baseline (58.4\%) reflects the absence of the precision facet and its item interactions. Five additional robustness analyses (estimator comparison, CI method, facet specification, leave-one-model-out, and null calibration) are reported in Appendix~\ref{app:robustness}; all confirm the stability of the reported variance structure.
144	
145	% [PLACEHOLDER: §4.7 Extended Results — 8-model × 5-benchmark expansion]
146

Binary correctness (1/0) serves as the primary outcome measure throughout; generation-based scoring avoids the log-likelihood artifacts identified by \\citet{hua2025flaw}.\n\n\\subsection{Single-Model Baseline}\n\nWe first establish the variance structure within a single model (Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct) on MMLU to isolate methodological facet contributions before introducing cross-model variation.\n\n\\paragraph{Design.} The G-study crosses six facets: 200 items $\\times$ 3 numerical precisions (BF16, FP16, FP32) $\\times$ 6 prompt templates $\\times$ 6 random seeds $\\times$ 3 temperature levels (0.0, 0.3, 0.7) $\\times$ 4 answer orderings, yielding 259,200 binary observations.     "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related}\n\n\\paragraph{Variance Decomposition in ML/NLP Evaluation.}\nFactorial decomposition of evaluation variance has roots in the ML reproducibility literature. \\citet{bouthillier2021accounting} introduced an ANOVA-based template that decomposes benchmark variance across implementation details, hyperparameters, and data splits, establishing that design choices account for a substantial share of reproducibility failures. Several subsequent studies applied similar decomposition logic to specific facets of model evaluation \\citep{dodge2020finetuning, henderson2018deep, magnusson2023reproducibility}. \\citet{mesting2026hidden} brought Generalizability theory into LLM evaluation, decomposing variance across five methodology facets on a single benchmark; judge identity dominated at 43.8\\%, with prompt$\\times$judge interaction at 12.4\\%. However, this study used a single benchmark, included no infrastructure facets such as numerical precision, and fixed the judge as an LLM evaluator rather than isolating other sources. \\citet{kunievsky2025intent} proposed an alternative decomposition that separates intent uncertainty, articulation variance, and model uncertainty, but this framework does not cross infrastructure and methodology facets in a factorial design. Our work extends these approaches by crossing seven facets across four benchmarks, enabling direct comparison of variance structures across evaluation formats.\n\n\\paragraph{Infrastructure and Prompt Variance.}\nA parallel line of work has examined individual variance sources in isolation. On the infrastructure side, \\citet{yuan2025layercast} documented up to 9\\% accuracy variation from numerical precision and GPU type but provided no factorial attribution. \\citet{messina2026background} formalized the concept of background temperature from floating-point non-associativity in GPU computation, and \\citet{thinkingmachines2025batch} proposed batch-invariant kernels to mitigate CUDA nondeterminism. On the methodology side, \\citet{sclar2024formatting} found up to 76\\% accuracy swings from prompt formatting changes on fixed hardware, and BrittleBench \\citep{brittlebench2026} reported 50\\% variance from prompt perturbation. \\citet{pezeshkpour2024ordering} showed that answer ordering in multiple-choice benchmarks introduces systematic bias, while \\citet{bui2025seeds} and \\citet{biderman2024lessons} documented sensitivity to random seeds and training configurations. Each of these studies isolates one factor while holding others fixed, which precludes estimating interactions between sources or comparing their relative magnitudes within a unified design.\n\n\\paragraph{IRT and Efficient Benchmarking.}\nItem Response Theory (IRT) offers a complementary perspective on evaluation design by modeling item difficulty and discrimination parameters. \\citet{lalor2019irt} introduced IRT-based item analysis for NLP benchmarks, and \\citet{rodriguez2021leaderboards} showed that IRT reveals hidden properties of evaluation items that raw accuracy scores obscure. Building on this foundation, \\citet{polo2024tinybenchmarks} developed tinyBenchmarks, using IRT-based subsampling to reduce evaluation cost while preserving ranking accuracy, and \\citet{zhou2026irt} recommended a 116-item subset via PSN-IRT for efficient MMLU evaluation. \\citet{perlitz2023efficient} explored related strategies for benchmark compression. These approaches optimize item selection for latent trait estimation but do not model variance from prompt, seed, or ordering facets. IRT-selected items evaluated under a single methodological condition can therefore systematically underestimate the number of items required for reliable measurement across diverse evaluation settings.\n\n\\paragraph{Binary Data and Psychometrics Foundations.}\nOur application of G-theory to binary correctness scores draws on established psychometric methodology. \\citet{shavelson1991generalizability} and \\citet{brennan2001generalizability} provide the canonical framework for multi-facet reliability analysis. \\citet{mushquash2006gtheory} extended G-theory to binary data, and \\citet{jiang2018bayesian} developed logistic-link generalized G-theory for binary outcomes. \\citet{tuerlinckx2006glmm} established the connection between generalized linear mixed models and G-theory variance components. These methods ground our use of Henderson Method~I for binary correctness scores (\\S\\ref{sec:binary}).\n\n\\paragraph{Positioning.}\nRelative to \\citet{mesting2026hidden}, we fix the judge (generation-based scoring) to isolate the remaining facets; their judge-dominated variance (43.8\\%) would otherwise mask all other sources. Our multi-benchmark design reveals format-conditional structure that single-benchmark studies cannot detect. Relative to IRT approaches \\citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks, zhou2026irt}, G-theory and IRT optimize orthogonal dimensions of evaluation design: IRT selects informative items, while G-theory determines the conditions needed for a target reliability. Our D-study quantifies this complementarity: multi-facet designs require [TBD-RATIO]$\\times$ fewer items than single-condition protocols for equivalent reliability. {
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "8e0c453a-157d-49d1-b99e-cd39a1f9f4b2",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "e18068a2-89ff-48e1-97c4-b5364069a907",

  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519/projects/nlp_variance_gtheory/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "[TBD-RATIO]$\\times$",
    "newString": "10.0$\\times$",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nOn multiple-choice benchmarks, item selection drives 42--49\\% of evaluation variance while prompt wording is negligible ($<$1\\%). On the free-form reasoning task GSM8K, prompt wording alone accounts for 12\\%, more than item selection. These numbers, drawn from our factorial analysis of 1.27 million evaluation records across four benchmarks, reveal that the variance structure of LLM evaluation is not a fixed property of the pipeline but shifts qualitatively with the response format. Current evaluation practice treats variance sources as independent nuisance factors and controls them one at a time: one study perturbs prompts, another varies precision, a third changes item subsets. This piecemeal approach cannot capture interactions between sources, nor can it predict how the relative importance of each source changes across evaluation settings. Leaderboard rankings built on single-condition evaluations are therefore sensitive to methodological choices that remain unreported and uncontrolled \\citep{rodriguez2021leaderboards, dehghani2021benchmark}. No unified factorial framework exists to decompose these sources simultaneously, quantify their interactions, and compare the resulting variance structure across benchmarks.\n\nExisting studies have examined individual variance sources in isolation, each implicitly assuming separability. \\citet{sclar2024formatting} documented up to 76\\% accuracy swings from prompt formatting perturbations but held hardware and item selection fixed. BrittleBench \\citep{brittlebench2026} found 50\\% variance from prompt perturbation under the same constraint. \\citet{yuan2025layercast} showed that numerical precision and GPU type cause up to 9\\% accuracy variation but provided no factorial attribution to specific sources or their interactions. \\citet{mesting2026hidden} applied Generalizability theory (G-theory) to LLM evaluation and decomposed variance across five methodology facets on a single benchmark; judge identity dominated at 43.8\\%, but the study included no cross-benchmark comparison and excluded infrastructure facets such as numerical precision. Studies of answer ordering \\citep{pezeshkpour2024ordering} and hyperparameter sensitivity \\citep{dodge2020finetuning, henderson2018deep} have documented additional sources of variation, again in isolation from the factors above. Item Response Theory (IRT) approaches \\citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks, lalor2019irt} model item difficulty parameters to construct efficient benchmarks but do not account for prompt, seed, or ordering facets. \\citet{bouthillier2021accounting} established ANOVA-based variance decomposition as a principled template for ML reproducibility, but this work predates the LLM evaluation context where benchmark format introduces an additional structural variable. Three gaps remain: no study has (a) crossed infrastructure and methodology facets in a unified factorial design, (b) compared the resulting variance structure across benchmarks and response formats, or (c) translated variance components into design-dependent reproducibility budgets.\n\nWe address these gaps with a multi-benchmark G-theory study \\citep{shavelson1991generalizability, brennan2001generalizability} that crosses seven facets (item, model, numerical precision, prompt template, random seed, sampling temperature, and answer ordering) across four models and four benchmarks (MMLU, ARC-Challenge, HellaSwag, and GSM8K), totaling 1.27 million records. The central finding is that variance structure is format-conditional. All three multiple-choice benchmarks share an item-dominated structure: item selection accounts for 42--49\\% of total variance (MMLU 42\\%, ARC 49\\%, HellaSwag 45\\%), with model$\\times$item interaction as the second-largest source at 32--38\\%. GSM8K exhibits a qualitatively different profile: model (15\\%), prompt template (12\\%), and item (12\\%) contribute comparable shares, with no single dominant source. This is a dichotomy, not a gradient. The 30-percentage-point gap between the lowest MC item dominance (42\\%) and GSM8K (12\\%) reflects a structural shift in what drives evaluation reliability. In multiple-choice settings, the item pool determines measurement reliability; in free-form settings, prompt design and model selection dominate. A second finding is that model$\\times$item interaction (33.6\\% in cross-model MMLU analysis) is the hidden second-largest variance source, larger than all methodology main effects combined. Models differ not in overall ability but in which specific items they find difficult. D-study projections translate these findings into practical design guidance: the format-conditional structure implies that reliability optimization strategies must differ between MC and free-form benchmarks. Figure~\\ref{fig:cross_benchmark} visualizes the contrast across all four benchmarks.\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Format-conditional variance structure.} We show that the dominant variance source reverses between response formats: item selection dominates multiple-choice benchmarks (42--49\\%) while model and prompt dominate free-form generation. Model$\\times$item interaction (33.6\\%) is the hidden second-largest source in cross-model analysis. D-study cross-validation confirms the structure (predicted $G = 0.816$, observed $G = 0.820$).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Precision separability.} Under binary scoring, BF16 and FP16 yield functionally identical results ($<$0.1\\% interaction with all other facets). This separability is metric-conditional: text-exact-match scoring reveals a 1.3\\% precision$\\times$temperature interaction absent under binary scoring.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Design-dependent reproducibility budgets.} Multi-facet evaluation designs require [TBD-RATIO]$\\times$ fewer items than single-condition designs to achieve $G \\geq 0.80$ (56 versus 631 items). IRT-selected 116-item subsets yield $G = 0.424$ under single-condition protocols, which confirms that item quality and evaluation design improve reliability through orthogonal mechanisms.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Open-source G-theory pipeline and D-study calculator.} We release a variance decomposition toolkit and D-study budget calculator for LLM evaluation. Practitioners can compute the number of items, models, and conditions needed for a target reliability before data collection.\n\\end{enumerate}",

          " ",

IRT-selected 116-item subsets yield $G = 0.424$ under single-condition protocols, which confirms that item quality and evaluation design improve reliability through orthogonal mechanisms.",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nThis study establishes that the variance structure of LLM evaluation is format-conditional. Across three multiple-choice benchmarks, item selection accounts for 42--49\\% of total variance, while on the free-form benchmark GSM8K, model (15\\%), prompt (12\\%), and item (12\\%) contribute comparable shares with no single dominant source. This is a qualitative dichotomy, not a gradient. Model$\\times$item interaction (33.6\\%) is the hidden second-largest source: models differ in which items they find difficult, not in overall ability. Our cross-benchmark D-study framework extends the single-benchmark G-theory approach of \\citet{mesting2026hidden} to format-conditional comparison and actionable design budgets.\n\nThe format-conditional structure implies distinct optimization strategies. For multiple-choice benchmarks, item pool composition is the primary reliability lever; varying prompts or seeds contributes negligible variance. For free-form benchmarks, prompt design and model selection matter as much as item selection, and evaluation protocols should systematically vary both. Across formats, multi-facet designs are [TBD-RATIO]$\\times$ more efficient than single-condition designs (56 versus 631 items for $G \\geq 0.80$). BF16 and FP16 are interchangeable under binary scoring on single-GPU inference, which eliminates precision as a required control variable. The released D-study calculator enables practitioners to determine the number of items, models, and conditions needed for a target reliability before data collection.\n\nThree extensions are planned. An 8-model expansion will strengthen the quasi-random model facet assumption by sampling a broader population of instruction-tuned architectures. Multi-GPU tensor parallelism will test whether precision separability holds under distributed numerical non-associativity.     "originalFile": "Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation results vary across reproductions, but variance sources interact, and their relative importance shifts with the benchmark format. No unified factorial framework exists to decompose these sources simultaneously across benchmarks. We apply Generalizability theory (G-theory) in a crossed design with seven facets across four models and four benchmarks, totaling 1.27 million evaluation records. The variance structure is format-conditional: on multiple-choice benchmarks, item selection accounts for 42--49\\% of total variance, while on the free-form benchmark GSM8K, model and prompt template each contribute 12--15\\% with no single dominant source. Model$\\times$item interaction (33.6\\%) is the hidden second-largest component, larger than all methodology main effects combined. Under binary scoring, BF16 and FP16 numerical precisions are functionally interchangeable ($<$0.1\\% interaction). Multi-facet designs require 10.0$\\times$ fewer items than single-condition designs to reach a generalizability coefficient of 0.80 (56 versus 631 items). We release an open-source G-theory pipeline and Decision study (D-study) budget calculator for evaluation design optimization.",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nOn multiple-choice benchmarks, item selection drives 42--49\\% of evaluation variance while prompt wording is negligible ($<$1\\%). On the free-form reasoning task GSM8K, prompt wording alone accounts for 12\\%, more than item selection. These numbers, drawn from our factorial analysis of 1.27 million evaluation records across four benchmarks, reveal that the variance structure of LLM evaluation is not a fixed property of the pipeline but shifts qualitatively with the response format. Current evaluation practice treats variance sources as independent nuisance factors and controls them one at a time: one study perturbs prompts, another varies precision, a third changes item subsets. This piecemeal approach cannot capture interactions between sources, nor can it predict how the relative importance of each source changes across evaluation settings. Leaderboard rankings built on single-condition evaluations are therefore sensitive to methodological choices that remain unreported and uncontrolled \\citep{rodriguez2021leaderboards, dehghani2021benchmark}. No unified factorial framework exists to decompose these sources simultaneously, quantify their interactions, and compare the resulting variance structure across benchmarks.\n\nExisting studies have examined individual variance sources in isolation, each implicitly assuming separability. \\citet{sclar2024formatting} documented up to 76\\% accuracy swings from prompt formatting perturbations but held hardware and item selection fixed. BrittleBench \\citep{brittlebench2026} found 50\\% variance from prompt perturbation under the same constraint. \\citet{yuan2025layercast} showed that numerical precision and GPU type cause up to 9\\% accuracy variation but provided no factorial attribution to specific sources or their interactions. \\citet{mesting2026hidden} applied Generalizability theory (G-theory) to LLM evaluation and decomposed variance across five methodology facets on a single benchmark; judge identity dominated at 43.8\\%, but the study included no cross-benchmark comparison and excluded infrastructure facets such as numerical precision. Studies of answer ordering \\citep{pezeshkpour2024ordering} and hyperparameter sensitivity \\citep{dodge2020finetuning, henderson2018deep} have documented additional sources of variation, again in isolation from the factors above. Item Response Theory (IRT) approaches \\citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks, lalor2019irt} model item difficulty parameters to construct efficient benchmarks but do not account for prompt, seed, or ordering facets. \\citet{bouthillier2021accounting} established ANOVA-based variance decomposition as a principled template for ML reproducibility, but this work predates the LLM evaluation context where benchmark format introduces an additional structural variable. Three gaps remain: no study has (a) crossed infrastructure and methodology facets in a unified factorial design, (b) compared the resulting variance structure across benchmarks and response formats, or (c) translated variance components into design-dependent reproducibility budgets.\n\nWe address these gaps with a multi-benchmark G-theory study \\citep{shavelson1991generalizability, brennan2001generalizability} that crosses seven facets (item, model, numerical precision, prompt template, random seed, sampling temperature, and answer ordering) across four models and four benchmarks (MMLU, ARC-Challenge, HellaSwag, and GSM8K), totaling 1.27 million records. The central finding is that variance structure is format-conditional. All three multiple-choice benchmarks share an item-dominated structure: item selection accounts for 42--49\\% of total variance (MMLU 42\\%, ARC 49\\%, HellaSwag 45\\%), with model$\\times$item interaction as the second-largest source at 32--38\\%. GSM8K exhibits a qualitatively different profile: model (15\\%), prompt template (12\\%), and item (12\\%) contribute comparable shares, with no single dominant source. This is a dichotomy, not a gradient. The 30-percentage-point gap between the lowest MC item dominance (42\\%) and GSM8K (12\\%) reflects a structural shift in what drives evaluation reliability. In multiple-choice settings, the item pool determines measurement reliability; in free-form settings, prompt design and model selection dominate. A second finding is that model$\\times$item interaction (33.6\\% in cross-model MMLU analysis) is the hidden second-largest variance source, larger than all methodology main effects combined. Models differ not in overall ability but in which specific items they find difficult. D-study projections translate these findings into practical design guidance: the format-conditional structure implies that reliability optimization strategies must differ between MC and free-form benchmarks. Figure~\\ref{fig:cross_benchmark} visualizes the contrast across all four benchmarks.\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Format-conditional variance structure.} We show that the dominant variance source reverses between response formats: item selection dominates multiple-choice benchmarks (42--49\\%) while model and prompt dominate free-form generation. Model$\\times$item interaction (33.6\\%) is the hidden second-largest source in cross-model analysis. D-study cross-validation confirms the structure (predicted $G = 0.816$, observed $G = 0.820$).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Precision separability.} Under binary scoring, BF16 and FP16 yield functionally identical results ($<$0.1\\% interaction with all other facets). This separability is metric-conditional: text-exact-match scoring reveals a 1.3\\% precision$\\times$temperature interaction absent under binary scoring.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Design-dependent reproducibility budgets.} Multi-facet evaluation designs require 10.0$\\times$ fewer items than single-condition designs to achieve $G \\geq 0.80$ (56 versus 631 items). IRT-selected 116-item subsets yield $G = 0.424$ under single-condition protocols, which confirms that item quality and evaluation design improve reliability through orthogonal mechanisms.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Open-source G-theory pipeline and D-study calculator.} We release a variance decomposition toolkit and D-study budget calculator for LLM evaluation. Practitioners can compute the number of items, models, and conditions needed for a target reliability before data collection.\n\\end{enumerate}",
