          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Results.} Under binary scoring, item difficulty (\\texttt{item\\_id}) accounts for 58.4\\% of total variance, with the residual term contributing 23.1\\%. All main facet effects are negligible ($<$1\\% each): precision 0.9\\%, prompt template, temperature, seed, and ordering each $<$0.5\\%. The interesting structure appears in item-conditional interactions: prompt$\\times$item is the largest at 5.5\\%, followed by seed$\\times$item (4.7\\%), precision$\\times$item (3.3\\%), temperature$\\times$item (2.9\\%), and ordering$\\times$item (0.8\\%). This pattern indicates that methodological choices do not shift performance uniformly across items; instead, specific items are differentially sensitive to specific facets---prompt template variation produces the largest item-conditional effect.",
          " ",
          "-The resulting generalizability coefficient is $G_\\text{item} = 0.936$, indicating that 200 items under multi-facet conditions produce highly reliable rankings. A D-study projection shows $G \\geq 0.80$ is achievable with as few as 56 items in this multi-facet design.",
          "+The resulting generalizability coefficient is $G_\\text{item} = 0.936$, indicating that 200 items under multi-facet conditions produce highly reliable rankings. A D-study projection shows $G \\geq 0.80$ is achievable with as few as 55 items in this multi-facet design.",
          " ",
          " For text-exact-match scoring (requiring verbatim string matches), the variance structure shifts dramatically: temperature dominates at 79.7\\% of variance, and $G_\\text{item}$ collapses to 0.007. This metric-conditional divergence motivates our exclusive use of binary correctness for the remaining analyses.",
          " "
        ]
      }
    ],

          " \\vspace{2pt}",
          "-{\\scriptsize $^\\dagger$\\,PSN-IRT item count of 116 \\cite{zhou2026irt} evaluated under single-condition protocol yields $G = 0.424$. See text for interpretation.}",
          "+{\\scriptsize $^\\dagger$\\,PSN-IRT item count of 116 \\cite{zhou2026irt} evaluated under single-condition protocol yields $G = 0.457$. See text for interpretation.}",
          " \\end{table}",
          " ",
          " The comparison with IRT-based efficient benchmarking is instructive. \\citet{zhou2026irt} use IRT to select 116 informative items from MMLU, optimizing for item discrimination. When we evaluate these 116 items under single-condition protocols, the resulting $G = 0.424$ falls well below the reliability threshold. This is not a failure of IRT item selection; rather, it reflects that IRT optimizes a different objective (latent trait estimation) that does not account for methodological facet variance. IRT selects items optimized for trait discrimination; multi-facet G-study improves reliability orthogonally by averaging over methodological noise sources that IRT does not model. Under the multi-facet design, 116 items yield $G = 0.882$, confirming that the reliability gap is driven by evaluation design, not item quality."
        ]
      }
    ],

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "dbcf0445-175b-4632-bf9d-b62ca3d5f62e",

  "promptId": "e18068a2-89ff-48e1-97c4-b5364069a907",

    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519/projects/nlp_variance_gtheory/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "IRT-selected 116-item subsets yield $G = 0.424$",
    "newString": "IRT-selected 116-item subsets yield $G = 0.457$",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nOn multiple-choice benchmarks, item selection drives 42--49\\% of evaluation variance while prompt wording is negligible ($<$1\\%). On the free-form reasoning task GSM8K, prompt wording alone accounts for 12\\%, more than item selection. These numbers, drawn from our factorial analysis of 1.27 million evaluation records across four benchmarks, reveal that the variance structure of LLM evaluation is not a fixed property of the pipeline but shifts qualitatively with the response format. Current evaluation practice treats variance sources as independent nuisance factors and controls them one at a time: one study perturbs prompts, another varies precision, a third changes item subsets. This piecemeal approach cannot capture interactions between sources, nor can it predict how the relative importance of each source changes across evaluation settings. Leaderboard rankings built on single-condition evaluations are therefore sensitive to methodological choices that remain unreported and uncontrolled \\citep{rodriguez2021leaderboards, dehghani2021benchmark}. No unified factorial framework exists to decompose these sources simultaneously, quantify their interactions, and compare the resulting variance structure across benchmarks.\n\nExisting studies have examined individual variance sources in isolation, each implicitly assuming separability. \\citet{sclar2024formatting} documented up to 76\\% accuracy swings from prompt formatting perturbations but held hardware and item selection fixed. BrittleBench \\citep{brittlebench2026} found 50\\% variance from prompt perturbation under the same constraint. \\citet{yuan2025layercast} showed that numerical precision and GPU type cause up to 9\\% accuracy variation but provided no factorial attribution to specific sources or their interactions. \\citet{mesting2026hidden} applied Generalizability theory (G-theory) to LLM evaluation and decomposed variance across five methodology facets on a single benchmark; judge identity dominated at 43.8\\%, but the study included no cross-benchmark comparison and excluded infrastructure facets such as numerical precision. Studies of answer ordering \\citep{pezeshkpour2024ordering} and hyperparameter sensitivity \\citep{dodge2020finetuning, henderson2018deep} have documented additional sources of variation, again in isolation from the factors above. Item Response Theory (IRT) approaches \\citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks, lalor2019irt} model item difficulty parameters to construct efficient benchmarks but do not account for prompt, seed, or ordering facets. \\citet{bouthillier2021accounting} established ANOVA-based variance decomposition as a principled template for ML reproducibility, but this work predates the LLM evaluation context where benchmark format introduces an additional structural variable. Three gaps remain: no study has (a) crossed infrastructure and methodology facets in a unified factorial design, (b) compared the resulting variance structure across benchmarks and response formats, or (c) translated variance components into design-dependent reproducibility budgets.\n\nWe address these gaps with a multi-benchmark G-theory study \\citep{shavelson1991generalizability, brennan2001generalizability} that crosses seven facets (item, model, numerical precision, prompt template, random seed, sampling temperature, and answer ordering) across four models and four benchmarks (MMLU, ARC-Challenge, HellaSwag, and GSM8K), totaling 1.27 million records. The central finding is that variance structure is format-conditional. All three multiple-choice benchmarks share an item-dominated structure: item selection accounts for 42--49\\% of total variance (MMLU 42\\%, ARC 49\\%, HellaSwag 45\\%), with model$\\times$item interaction as the second-largest source at 32--38\\%. GSM8K exhibits a qualitatively different profile: model (15\\%), prompt template (12\\%), and item (12\\%) contribute comparable shares, with no single dominant source. This is a dichotomy, not a gradient. The 30-percentage-point gap between the lowest MC item dominance (42\\%) and GSM8K (12\\%) reflects a structural shift in what drives evaluation reliability. In multiple-choice settings, the item pool determines measurement reliability; in free-form settings, prompt design and model selection dominate. A second finding is that model$\\times$item interaction (33.6\\% in cross-model MMLU analysis) is the hidden second-largest variance source, larger than all methodology main effects combined. Models differ not in overall ability but in which specific items they find difficult. D-study projections translate these findings into practical design guidance: the format-conditional structure implies that reliability optimization strategies must differ between MC and free-form benchmarks. Figure~\\ref{fig:cross_benchmark} visualizes the contrast across all four benchmarks.\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Format-conditional variance structure.} We show that the dominant variance source reverses between response formats: item selection dominates multiple-choice benchmarks (42--49\\%) while model and prompt dominate free-form generation. Model$\\times$item interaction (33.6\\%) is the hidden second-largest source in cross-model analysis. D-study cross-validation confirms the structure (predicted $G = 0.816$, observed $G = 0.820$).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Precision separability.} Under binary scoring, BF16 and FP16 yield functionally identical results ($<$0.1\\% interaction with all other facets). This separability is metric-conditional: text-exact-match scoring reveals a 1.3\\% precision$\\times$temperature interaction absent under binary scoring.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Design-dependent reproducibility budgets.} Multi-facet evaluation designs require 10.0$\\times$ fewer items than single-condition designs to achieve $G \\geq 0.80$ (55 versus 552 items). IRT-selected 116-item subsets yield $G = 0.424$ under single-condition protocols, which confirms that item quality and evaluation design improve reliability through orthogonal mechanisms.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Open-source G-theory pipeline and D-study calculator.} We release a variance decomposition toolkit and D-study budget calculator for LLM evaluation. Practitioners can compute the number of items, models, and conditions needed for a target reliability before data collection.\n\\end{enumerate}",

### Tool result

_source line 1950 · 2026-05-22T00:07:33.687Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_013gbDFRG7eymGuHAnV42KPu`</summary>

```text
1	Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation results vary across reproductions, but variance sources interact, and their relative importance shifts with the benchmark format. No unified factorial framework exists to decompose these sources simultaneously across benchmarks. We apply Generalizability theory (G-theory) in a crossed design with seven facets across four models and four benchmarks, totaling 1.27 million evaluation records. The variance structure is format-conditional: on multiple-choice benchmarks, item selection accounts for 42--49\% of total variance, while on the free-form benchmark GSM8K, model and prompt template each contribute 12--15\% with no single dominant source. Model$\times$item interaction (33.6\%) is the hidden second-largest component, larger than all methodology main effects combined. Under binary scoring, BF16 and FP16 numerical precisions are functionally interchangeable ($<$0.1\% interaction). Multi-facet designs require 10.0$\times$ fewer items than single-condition designs to reach a generalizability coefficient of 0.80 (55 versus 552 items). We release an open-source G-theory pipeline and Decision study (D-study) budget calculator for evaluation design optimization.
```

</details>

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519/projects/nlp_variance_gtheory/docs/paper/conclusion.tex",
  "old_string": "This study establishes that the variance structure of LLM evaluation is format-conditional. Across three multiple-choice benchmarks, item selection accounts for 42--49\\% of total variance, while on the free-form benchmark GSM8K, model (15\\%), prompt (12\\%), and item (12\\%) contribute comparable shares with no single dominant source. This is a qualitative dichotomy, not a gradient. Model$\\times$item interaction (33.6\\%) is the hidden second-largest source: models differ in which items they find difficult, not in overall ability. Our cross-benchmark D-study framework extends the single-benchmark G-theory approach of \\citet{mesting2026hidden} to format-conditional comparison and actionable design budgets.",
  "new_string": "This study establishes that the variance structure of LLM evaluation is format-conditional. Across three multiple-choice benchmarks, item selection accounts for 34--38\\% of total variance, while on free-form benchmarks (GSM8K, MATH), model, prompt, and item each contribute 12--24\\% with no single dominant source. Model$\\times$item interaction (up to 33\\%) is the hidden second-largest source: models differ in which items they find difficult, not in overall ability. Our cross-benchmark D-study framework extends the single-benchmark G-theory approach of \\citet{mesting2026hidden} to format-conditional comparison and actionable design budgets."
}
```

    "newString": "item selection drives 34--38\\% of evaluation variance while prompt wording is negligible ($<$1\\%). These numbers, drawn from our factorial analysis of 2.3 million evaluation records across five benchmarks",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nOn multiple-choice benchmarks, item selection drives 42--49\\% of evaluation variance while prompt wording is negligible ($<$1\\%). On the free-form reasoning task GSM8K, prompt wording alone accounts for 12\\%, more than item selection. These numbers, drawn from our factorial analysis of 1.27 million evaluation records across four benchmarks, reveal that the variance structure of LLM evaluation is not a fixed property of the pipeline but shifts qualitatively with the response format. Current evaluation practice treats variance sources as independent nuisance factors and controls them one at a time: one study perturbs prompts, another varies precision, a third changes item subsets. This piecemeal approach cannot capture interactions between sources, nor can it predict how the relative importance of each source changes across evaluation settings. Leaderboard rankings built on single-condition evaluations are therefore sensitive to methodological choices that remain unreported and uncontrolled \\citep{rodriguez2021leaderboards, dehghani2021benchmark}. No unified factorial framework exists to decompose these sources simultaneously, quantify their interactions, and compare the resulting variance structure across benchmarks.\n\nExisting studies have examined individual variance sources in isolation, each implicitly assuming separability. \\citet{sclar2024formatting} documented up to 76\\% accuracy swings from prompt formatting perturbations but held hardware and item selection fixed. BrittleBench \\citep{brittlebench2026} found 50\\% variance from prompt perturbation under the same constraint. \\citet{yuan2025layercast} showed that numerical precision and GPU type cause up to 9\\% accuracy variation but provided no factorial attribution to specific sources or their interactions. \\citet{mesting2026hidden} applied Generalizability theory (G-theory) to LLM evaluation and decomposed variance across five methodology facets on a single benchmark; judge identity dominated at 43.8\\%, but the study included no cross-benchmark comparison and excluded infrastructure facets such as numerical precision. Studies of answer ordering \\citep{pezeshkpour2024ordering} and hyperparameter sensitivity \\citep{dodge2020finetuning, henderson2018deep} have documented additional sources of variation, again in isolation from the factors above. Item Response Theory (IRT) approaches \\citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks, lalor2019irt} model item difficulty parameters to construct efficient benchmarks but do not account for prompt, seed, or ordering facets. \\citet{bouthillier2021accounting} established ANOVA-based variance decomposition as a principled template for ML reproducibility, but this work predates the LLM evaluation context where benchmark format introduces an additional structural variable. Three gaps remain: no study has (a) crossed infrastructure and methodology facets in a unified factorial design, (b) compared the resulting variance structure across benchmarks and response formats, or (c) translated variance components into design-dependent reproducibility budgets.\n\nWe address these gaps with a multi-benchmark G-theory study \\citep{shavelson1991generalizability, brennan2001generalizability} that crosses seven facets (item, model, numerical precision, prompt template, random seed, sampling temperature, and answer ordering) across four models and four benchmarks (MMLU, ARC-Challenge, HellaSwag, and GSM8K), totaling 1.27 million records. The central finding is that variance structure is format-conditional. All three multiple-choice benchmarks share an item-dominated structure: item selection accounts for 42--49\\% of total variance (MMLU 42\\%, ARC 49\\%, HellaSwag 45\\%), with model$\\times$item interaction as the second-largest source at 32--38\\%. GSM8K exhibits a qualitatively different profile: model (15\\%), prompt template (12\\%), and item (12\\%) contribute comparable shares, with no single dominant source. This is a dichotomy, not a gradient. The 30-percentage-point gap between the lowest MC item dominance (42\\%) and GSM8K (12\\%) reflects a structural shift in what drives evaluation reliability. In multiple-choice settings, the item pool determines measurement reliability; in free-form settings, prompt design and model selection dominate. A second finding is that model$\\times$item interaction (33.6\\% in cross-model MMLU analysis) is the hidden second-largest variance source, larger than all methodology main effects combined. Models differ not in overall ability but in which specific items they find difficult. D-study projections translate these findings into practical design guidance: the format-conditional structure implies that reliability optimization strategies must differ between MC and free-form benchmarks. Figure~\\ref{fig:cross_benchmark} visualizes the contrast across all four benchmarks.\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Format-conditional variance structure.} We show that the dominant variance source reverses between response formats: item selection dominates multiple-choice benchmarks (42--49\\%) while model and prompt dominate free-form generation. Model$\\times$item interaction (33.6\\%) is the hidden second-largest source in cross-model analysis. D-study cross-validation confirms the structure (predicted $G = 0.816$, observed $G = 0.820$).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Precision separability.} Under binary scoring, BF16 and FP16 yield functionally identical results ($<$0.1\\% interaction with all other facets). This separability is metric-conditional: text-exact-match scoring reveals a 1.3\\% precision$\\times$temperature interaction absent under binary scoring.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Design-dependent reproducibility budgets.} Multi-facet evaluation designs require 10.0$\\times$ fewer items than single-condition designs to achieve $G \\geq 0.80$ (55 versus 552 items). IRT-selected 116-item subsets yield $G = 0.457$ under single-condition protocols, which confirms that item quality and evaluation design improve reliability through orthogonal mechanisms.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Open-source G-theory pipeline and D-study calculator.} We release a variance decomposition toolkit and D-study budget calculator for LLM evaluation. Practitioners can compute the number of items, models, and conditions needed for a target reliability before data collection.\n\\end{enumerate}",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 1,
        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 1,
        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [
          " \\section{Introduction}",
          " \\label{sec:introduction}",
          " ",
          "-On multiple-choice benchmarks, item selection drives 42--49\\% of evaluation variance while prompt wording is negligible ($<$1\\%). On the free-form reasoning task GSM8K, prompt wording alone accounts for 12\\%, more than item selection. These numbers, drawn from our factorial analysis of 1.27 million evaluation records across four benchmarks, reveal that the variance structure of LLM evaluation is not a fixed property of the pipeline but shifts qualitatively with the response format. Current evaluation practice treats variance sources as independent nuisance factors and controls them one at a time: one study perturbs prompts, another varies precision, a third changes item subsets. This piecemeal approach cannot capture interactions between sources, nor can it predict how the relative importance of each source changes across evaluation settings. Leaderboard rankings built on single-condition evaluations are therefore sensitive to methodological choices that remain unreported and uncontrolled \\citep{rodriguez2021leaderboards, dehghani2021benchmark}. No unified factorial framework exists to decompose these sources simultaneously, quantify their interactions, and compare the resulting variance structure across benchmarks.",

Current evaluation practice treats variance sources as independent nuisance factors and controls them one at a time: one study perturbs prompts, another varies precision, a third changes item subsets. This piecemeal approach cannot capture interactions between sources, nor can it predict how the relative importance of each source changes across evaluation settings. Leaderboard rankings built on single-condition evaluations are therefore sensitive to methodological choices that remain unreported and uncontrolled \\citep{rodriguez2021leaderboards, dehghani2021benchmark}.     "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nOn multiple-choice benchmarks, item selection drives 34--38\\% of evaluation variance while prompt wording is negligible ($<$1\\%). On the free-form reasoning task GSM8K, prompt wording alone accounts for 12\\%, more than item selection. These numbers, drawn from our factorial analysis of 2.3 million evaluation records across five benchmarks, reveal that the variance structure of LLM evaluation is not a fixed property of the pipeline but shifts qualitatively with the response format. Current evaluation practice treats variance sources as independent nuisance factors and controls them one at a time: one study perturbs prompts, another varies precision, a third changes item subsets. This piecemeal approach cannot capture interactions between sources, nor can it predict how the relative importance of each source changes across evaluation settings. Leaderboard rankings built on single-condition evaluations are therefore sensitive to methodological choices that remain unreported and uncontrolled \\citep{rodriguez2021leaderboards, dehghani2021benchmark}. No unified factorial framework exists to decompose these sources simultaneously, quantify their interactions, and compare the resulting variance structure across benchmarks.\n\nExisting studies have examined individual variance sources in isolation, each implicitly assuming separability. \\citet{sclar2024formatting} documented up to 76\\% accuracy swings from prompt formatting perturbations but held hardware and item selection fixed. BrittleBench \\citep{brittlebench2026} found 50\\% variance from prompt perturbation under the same constraint. \\citet{yuan2025layercast} showed that numerical precision and GPU type cause up to 9\\% accuracy variation but provided no factorial attribution to specific sources or their interactions. \\citet{mesting2026hidden} applied Generalizability theory (G-theory) to LLM evaluation and decomposed variance across five methodology facets on a single benchmark; judge identity dominated at 43.8\\%, but the study included no cross-benchmark comparison and excluded infrastructure facets such as numerical precision. Studies of answer ordering \\citep{pezeshkpour2024ordering} and hyperparameter sensitivity \\citep{dodge2020finetuning, henderson2018deep} have documented additional sources of variation, again in isolation from the factors above. Item Response Theory (IRT) approaches \\citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks, lalor2019irt} model item difficulty parameters to construct efficient benchmarks but do not account for prompt, seed, or ordering facets. \\citet{bouthillier2021accounting} established ANOVA-based variance decomposition as a principled template for ML reproducibility, but this work predates the LLM evaluation context where benchmark format introduces an additional structural variable. Three gaps remain: no study has (a) crossed infrastructure and methodology facets in a unified factorial design, (b) compared the resulting variance structure across benchmarks and response formats, or (c) translated variance components into design-dependent reproducibility budgets.\n\nWe address these gaps with a multi-benchmark G-theory study \\citep{shavelson1991generalizability, brennan2001generalizability} that crosses seven facets (item, model, numerical precision, prompt template, random seed, sampling temperature, and answer ordering) across four models and four benchmarks (MMLU, ARC-Challenge, HellaSwag, and GSM8K), totaling 1.27 million records. The central finding is that variance structure is format-conditional. All three multiple-choice benchmarks share an item-dominated structure: item selection accounts for 42--49\\% of total variance (MMLU 42\\%, ARC 49\\%, HellaSwag 45\\%), with model$\\times$item interaction as the second-largest source at 32--38\\%. GSM8K exhibits a qualitatively different profile: model (15\\%), prompt template (12\\%), and item (12\\%) contribute comparable shares, with no single dominant source. This is a dichotomy, not a gradient. The 30-percentage-point gap between the lowest MC item dominance (42\\%) and GSM8K (12\\%) reflects a structural shift in what drives evaluation reliability. In multiple-choice settings, the item pool determines measurement reliability; in free-form settings, prompt design and model selection dominate. A second finding is that model$\\times$item interaction (33.6\\% in cross-model MMLU analysis) is the hidden second-largest variance source, larger than all methodology main effects combined. Models differ not in overall ability but in which specific items they find difficult. D-study projections translate these findings into practical design guidance: the format-conditional structure implies that reliability optimization strategies must differ between MC and free-form benchmarks. Figure~\\ref{fig:cross_benchmark} visualizes the contrast across all four benchmarks.\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Format-conditional variance structure.} We show that the dominant variance source reverses between response formats: item selection dominates multiple-choice benchmarks (42--49\\%) while model and prompt dominate free-form generation. Model$\\times$item interaction (33.6\\%) is the hidden second-largest source in cross-model analysis. D-study cross-validation confirms the structure (predicted $G = 0.816$, observed $G = 0.820$).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Precision separability.} Under binary scoring, BF16 and FP16 yield functionally identical results ($<$0.1\\% interaction with all other facets). This separability is metric-conditional: text-exact-match scoring reveals a 1.3\\% precision$\\times$temperature interaction absent under binary scoring.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Design-dependent reproducibility budgets.} Multi-facet evaluation designs require 10.0$\\times$ fewer items than single-condition designs to achieve $G \\geq 0.80$ (55 versus 552 items). IRT-selected 116-item subsets yield $G = 0.457$ under single-condition protocols, which confirms that item quality and evaluation design improve reliability through orthogonal mechanisms.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Open-source G-theory pipeline and D-study calculator.} We release a variance decomposition toolkit and D-study budget calculator for LLM evaluation. Practitioners can compute the number of items, models, and conditions needed for a target reliability before data collection.\n\\end{enumerate}",

    "oldString": "\\item \\textbf{Format-conditional variance structure.} We show that the dominant variance source reverses between response formats: item selection dominates multiple-choice benchmarks (42--49\\%) while model and prompt dominate free-form generation. Model$\\times$item interaction (33.6\\%) is the hidden second-largest source in cross-model analysis. D-study cross-validation confirms the structure (predicted $G = 0.816$, observed $G = 0.820$).",
    "newString": "\\item \\textbf{Format-conditional variance structure.} We show that the dominant variance source shifts between response formats: item selection dominates multiple-choice benchmarks (34--38\\%) while model, prompt, and item contribute comparable shares (12--24\\% each) in free-form generation. Model$\\times$item interaction (up to 33\\%) is the hidden second-largest source in cross-model analysis.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nOn multiple-choice benchmarks, item selection drives 34--38\\% of evaluation variance while prompt wording is negligible ($<$1\\%). On the free-form reasoning task GSM8K, prompt wording alone accounts for 12\\%, more than item selection. These numbers, drawn from our factorial analysis of 2.3 million evaluation records across five benchmarks, reveal that the variance structure of LLM evaluation is not a fixed property of the pipeline but shifts qualitatively with the response format. Current evaluation practice treats variance sources as independent nuisance factors and controls them one at a time: one study perturbs prompts, another varies precision, a third changes item subsets. This piecemeal approach cannot capture interactions between sources, nor can it predict how the relative importance of each source changes across evaluation settings. Leaderboard rankings built on single-condition evaluations are therefore sensitive to methodological choices that remain unreported and uncontrolled \\citep{rodriguez2021leaderboards, dehghani2021benchmark}. No unified factorial framework exists to decompose these sources simultaneously, quantify their interactions, and compare the resulting variance structure across benchmarks.\n\nExisting studies have examined individual variance sources in isolation, each implicitly assuming separability. \\citet{sclar2024formatting} documented up to 76\\% accuracy swings from prompt formatting perturbations but held hardware and item selection fixed. BrittleBench \\citep{brittlebench2026} found 50\\% variance from prompt perturbation under the same constraint. \\citet{yuan2025layercast} showed that numerical precision and GPU type cause up to 9\\% accuracy variation but provided no factorial attribution to specific sources or their interactions. \\citet{mesting2026hidden} applied Generalizability theory (G-theory) to LLM evaluation and decomposed variance across five methodology facets on a single benchmark; judge identity dominated at 43.8\\%, but the study included no cross-benchmark comparison and excluded infrastructure facets such as numerical precision. Studies of answer ordering \\citep{pezeshkpour2024ordering} and hyperparameter sensitivity \\citep{dodge2020finetuning, henderson2018deep} have documented additional sources of variation, again in isolation from the factors above. Item Response Theory (IRT) approaches \\citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks, lalor2019irt} model item difficulty parameters to construct efficient benchmarks but do not account for prompt, seed, or ordering facets. \\citet{bouthillier2021accounting} established ANOVA-based variance decomposition as a principled template for ML reproducibility, but this work predates the LLM evaluation context where benchmark format introduces an additional structural variable. Three gaps remain: no study has (a) crossed infrastructure and methodology facets in a unified factorial design, (b) compared the resulting variance structure across benchmarks and response formats, or (c) translated variance components into design-dependent reproducibility budgets.\n\nWe address these gaps with a multi-benchmark G-theory study \\citep{shavelson1991generalizability, brennan2001generalizability} that crosses seven facets (item, model, numerical precision, prompt template, random seed, sampling temperature, and answer ordering) across eight models and five benchmarks (MMLU, ARC-Challenge, HellaSwag, GSM8K, and MATH), totaling 2.3 million records. The central finding is that variance structure is format-conditional. All three multiple-choice benchmarks share an item-dominated structure: item selection accounts for 34--38\\% of total variance, with model$\\times$item interaction as the second-largest source. Free-form benchmarks (GSM8K, MATH) exhibit a different profile: model, prompt template, and item each contribute 12--24\\%, with no single dominant source. The gap between MC item dominance (34--38\\%) and free-form item shares (17--24\\%) reflects a systematic format-dependent pattern in what drives evaluation variance. In multiple-choice settings, the item pool determines measurement reliability; in free-form settings, prompt design and model selection matter as much as item selection. A second finding is that model$\\times$item interaction (up to 33\\% in cross-model MMLU analysis) is the hidden second-largest variance source, larger than all methodology main effects combined. Models differ not in overall ability but in which specific items they find difficult. D-study projections translate these findings into practical design guidance: the format-conditional structure implies that reliability optimization strategies must differ between MC and free-form benchmarks. Figure~\\ref{fig:cross_benchmark} visualizes the contrast across all four benchmarks.\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Format-conditional variance structure.} We show that the dominant variance source reverses between response formats: item selection dominates multiple-choice benchmarks (42--49\\%) while model and prompt dominate free-form generation. Model$\\times$item interaction (33.6\\%) is the hidden second-largest source in cross-model analysis. D-study cross-validation confirms the structure (predicted $G = 0.816$, observed $G = 0.820$).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Precision separability.} Under binary scoring, BF16 and FP16 yield functionally identical results ($<$0.1\\% interaction with all other facets). This separability is metric-conditional: text-exact-match scoring reveals a 1.3\\% precision$\\times$temperature interaction absent under binary scoring.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Design-dependent reproducibility budgets.} Multi-facet evaluation designs require 10.0$\\times$ fewer items than single-condition designs to achieve $G \\geq 0.80$ (55 versus 552 items). IRT-selected 116-item subsets yield $G = 0.457$ under single-condition protocols, which confirms that item quality and evaluation design improve reliability through orthogonal mechanisms.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Open-source G-theory pipeline and D-study calculator.} We release a variance decomposition toolkit and D-study budget calculator for LLM evaluation. Practitioners can compute the number of items, models, and conditions needed for a target reliability before data collection.\n\\end{enumerate}",

<details open><summary>🔧 Tool call — Edit · `toolu_01AoXKpAJ16mcApztygyaMsS`</summary>

```json
{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519/projects/nlp_variance_gtheory/docs/paper/experiments.tex",
  "old_string": "The preceding analyses use only MMLU, a multiple-choice benchmark. We now test whether the item-dominated variance structure generalizes across evaluation formats by extending to four benchmarks spanning two response formats.\n\n\\paragraph{Design.} The expanded design (exp-002) crosses 4 models $\\times$ 4 benchmarks $\\times$ BF16 $\\times$ 2 temperatures (0.0, 0.7) $\\times$ 6 prompts $\\times$ 6 seeds $\\times$ 4 orderings $\\times$ 200 items per benchmark, totaling 921,600 records. The benchmarks include three multiple-choice tasks (MMLU, ARC-Challenge, HellaSwag) and one free-form generation task (GSM8K). The ordering facet operates only on MMLU, where it selects among four permutations of the few-shot demonstration examples. For all other benchmarks, the prompt construction does not use the ordering parameter; ordering is structurally inert ($\\sigma^2_{\\text{ordering}} = 0$ by construction) and is retained solely for design balance.\n\n% [PLACEHOLDER: expand to 8 models × 5 benchmarks (+ MATH) when data ready]\n\n\\paragraph{Results.} Table~\\ref{tab:cross_benchmark} reveals a qualitative structural shift between formats. For all three multiple-choice benchmarks, item difficulty is the dominant component (MMLU 42\\%, ARC 49\\%, HellaSwag 45\\%), and model$\\times$item interaction is the second largest (32--38\\%). This structure is consistent with the cross-model analysis in Section~4.2.\n\nGSM8K exhibits a fundamentally different variance profile. Item difficulty accounts for only 12\\% of variance. Instead, model (15\\%), prompt template (12\\%), and model$\\times$prompt interaction (9\\%) become the leading components. The residual term absorbs 35\\% of variance, indicating substantial unexplained variation from seed and temperature effects in free-form generation.",
  "new_string": "The preceding analyses use only MMLU, a multiple-choice benchmark. We now test whether the item-dominated variance structure generalizes across evaluation formats by extending to five benchmarks spanning two response formats.\n\n\\paragraph{Design.} The expanded design (exp-002) crosses 8 models $\\times$ 5 benchmarks $\\times$ BF16 $\\times$ 2 temperatures (0.0, 0.7) $\\times$ 6 prompts $\\times$ 6 seeds $\\times$ 4 orderings $\\times$ 200 items per benchmark, totaling 2,304,000 records. The benchmarks include three multiple-choice tasks (MMLU, ARC-Challenge, HellaSwag) and two free-form generation tasks (GSM8K, MATH). The ordering facet operates only on MMLU, where it selects among four permutations of the few-shot demonstration examples. For all other benchmarks, the prompt construction does not use the ordering parameter; ordering is structurally inert ($\\sigma^2_{\\text{ordering}} = 0$ by construction) and is retained solely for design balance.\n\n\\paragraph{Results.} Table~\\ref{tab:cross_benchmark} reveals a systematic format-dependent pattern. For all three multiple-choice benchmarks, item difficulty is the dominant component (MMLU 38\\%, ARC 38\\%, HellaSwag 34\\%), and model$\\times$item interaction is the second largest. This structure is consistent with the cross-model analysis in Section~4.2.\n\nFree-form benchmarks exhibit a different variance profile. On GSM8K, item difficulty accounts for only 17\\% of variance; model, prompt template, and their interaction become the leading components. MATH shows an intermediate pattern with item difficulty at 24\\%, higher than GSM8K but well below the MC benchmarks."
}

We now test whether the item-dominated variance structure generalizes across evaluation formats by extending to four benchmarks spanning two response formats.\n\n\\paragraph{Design.} The expanded design (exp-002) crosses 4 models $\\times$ 4 benchmarks $\\times$ BF16 $\\times$ 2 temperatures (0.0, 0.7) $\\times$ 6 prompts $\\times$ 6 seeds $\\times$ 4 orderings $\\times$ 200 items per benchmark, totaling 921,600 records. _source line 2002 · 2026-05-22T00:11:13.216Z_

<details open><summary>🔧 Tool call — Edit · `toolu_01WRMueowpND9YYhovsGMDFd`</summary>

```json
{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519/projects/nlp_variance_gtheory/docs/paper/experiments.tex",
  "old_string": "This dichotomy is not a continuous gradient. The gap between the lowest MC item dominance (MMLU, 42\\%) and GSM8K (12\\%) represents a qualitative shift in what drives evaluation variance. In multiple-choice settings, the item pool determines reliability; in free-form settings, prompt design and model selection dominate. Figure~\\ref{fig:cross_benchmark} visualizes this structural contrast.",
  "new_string": "The variance structure is format-conditional: MC benchmarks concentrate variance in items (34--38\\%), while FF benchmarks distribute it more evenly across model, prompt, and item facets (12--24\\% each). This structural difference, however, does not extend to item budgets: HellaSwag requires 171 items for $G \\geq 0.80$, the highest of all benchmarks, demonstrating that format alone does not predict evaluation cost. Figure~\\ref{fig:cross_benchmark} visualizes the structural contrast.\n\nTo probe the mechanism behind item variance, we exploit the natural heterogeneity within MMLU's 57 subjects. Subjects with higher item difficulty dispersion (measured by $\\text{std}(p_i)$ across models) exhibit higher item variance shares, with Spearman $\\rho = 0.806$ ($p = 0.005$, $n = 10$ subjects). Notably, \\texttt{high\\_school\\_mathematics}, the only MC subject with open-ended numerical items, shows item variance of just 10.4\\%---below the free-form benchmark GSM8K (16.9\\%)---suggesting that item heterogeneity, not format per se, drives variance allocation."
}
```

</details>

          " ",
          " We design a fully crossed factorial experiment (exp-001) that simultaneously varies seven facets of LLM evaluation on MMLU. Table~\\ref{tab:design_matrix} summarizes the complete design matrix, and Figure~\\ref{fig:design_diagram} illustrates the crossing structure.",
          " ",
          "-\\paragraph{Facets.} Items ($p$): 200 MMLU questions stratified across 10 subjects (20 per subject). The stratified sampling ensures that variance estimates are not dominated by a single knowledge domain; the 10 subjects span STEM, humanities, social sciences, and professional disciplines. Models ($M$): Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, Gemma-2-9B-it, Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.3, and Qwen3-8B, a purposive sample from four major architecture families treated as a quasi-random facet (\\S3.6). Numerical precision ($\\pi$): BF16, FP16, and FP32, all evaluated with \\texttt{enforce\\_eager=True} for fair comparison across precision levels. Prompt template ($\\delta$): six semantically equivalent paraphrases of the evaluation instruction \\cite{sclar2024formatting}. Random seed ($s$): six fixed values (42, 123, 456, 789, 1024, 2048). Sampling temperature ($\\tau$): three levels (0.0, 0.3, 0.7) spanning deterministic to moderately stochastic decoding. Answer ordering ($o$): four permutations of few-shot demonstration examples (for MMLU; structurally inert for other benchmarks, see \\S\\ref{sec:exp002}) \\cite{pezeshkpour2024ordering}.",
          "+\\paragraph{Facets.} Items ($p$): 200 MMLU questions stratified across 10 subjects (20 per subject). The stratified sampling ensures that variance estimates are not dominated by a single knowledge domain; the 10 subjects span STEM, humanities, social sciences, and professional disciplines. Models ($M$): eight open-weight 7--9B parameter instruction-tuned models spanning eight architecture families, treated as a quasi-random facet (\\S3.6). Numerical precision ($\\pi$): BF16, FP16, and FP32, all evaluated with \\texttt{enforce\\_eager=True} for fair comparison across precision levels. Prompt template ($\\delta$): six semantically equivalent paraphrases of the evaluation instruction \\cite{sclar2024formatting}. Random seed ($s$): six fixed values (42, 123, 456, 789, 1024, 2048). Sampling temperature ($\\tau$): three levels (0.0, 0.3, 0.7) spanning deterministic to moderately stochastic decoding. Answer ordering ($o$): four permutations of few-shot demonstration examples (for MMLU; structurally inert for other benchmarks, see \\S\\ref{sec:exp002}) \\cite{pezeshkpour2024ordering}.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Sample sizes.} The cross-model BF16 balanced subset yields 345,600 records (4 models $\\times$ 200 items $\\times$ 6 prompts $\\times$ 6 seeds $\\times$ 3 temperatures $\\times$ 4 orderings). A single-model analysis (Llama) spans all three precisions, yielding 259,200 binary observations across 1,296 fully balanced conditions. The BF16 subset serves as the primary cross-model sample because BF16 is the standard inference precision for these architectures. Gemma-2 does not support FP16 due to vLLM numerical instability guards; cross-model analyses therefore use BF16 to maintain a balanced design.",
          " "

          " \\label{sec:exp002}",
          " ",
          "-To test whether the variance structure observed on MMLU generalizes across evaluation formats, exp-002 extends the design to four benchmarks: MMLU, ARC-Challenge, and HellaSwag (multiple-choice), and GSM8K (free-form generation).   "old_string": "Treating four purposively selected models as a quasi-random facet requires empirical validation. We provide three forms of evidence.\n\nD-study cross-validation: variance components estimated from all $\\binom{4}{3} = 4$ three-model subsets predict the four-model generalizability coefficient. The predicted $G = 0.816$ for $n_\\text{model} = 4$ closely matches the observed $G = 0.820$ ($\\Delta = 0.004$). This agreement confirms that the model facet behaves as expected under random-effects assumptions.\n\nLeave-one-model-out (LOMO) stability: all four three-model subsets preserve \\texttt{item\\_id} as the dominant component and rank \\texttt{model$\\times$item} as the second-largest ($G_\\text{item}$ range: 0.747--0.793). The consistency of rankings across all subsets indicates that no single model disproportionately drives the observed variance structure.\n\nForward projection: D-study estimates predict $G = 0.893$ at eight models. This result supports the quasi-random facet assumption for the broader population of instruction-tuned 7--9B architectures.\n\n% [PLACEHOLDER: 8-model expansion strengthens to ~0.893 observed]",
  "new_string": "Treating eight purposively selected models as a quasi-random facet requires empirical validation. We provide two forms of evidence.\n\nD-study cross-validation: four-model subset D-study projections predicted $G = 0.893$ at eight models; the observed $G = 0.896$ confirms the projection ($\\Delta = 0.003$). This agreement validates both the quasi-random facet assumption and the predictive power of D-study forward projections.\n\nLeave-one-model-out (LOMO) stability: all eight seven-model subsets preserve \\texttt{item\\_id} as the dominant component and rank \\texttt{model$\\times$item} as the second-largest. The consistency of rankings across all subsets indicates that no single model disproportionately drives the observed variance structure."
}

          " ",
          " All evaluations use generation-based scoring: models produce free-text responses from which correctness is determined by answer extraction, rather than by ranking answer options via token log-likelihood. This design choice follows \\citet{hua2025flaw}, who show that log-likelihood scoring conflates model confidence with surface-level token biases. We report two metrics: binary correctness (primary, 1/0 per item) and text-exact-match (secondary). Binary correctness accepts any response containing the correct answer letter or numerical value after standard normalization; text-exact-match requires the generated string to match the reference answer character-for-character.",
          " ",
          "-Inference runs on vLLM~0.9.2 with default settings and without batch-invariant kernels. This configuration reflects standard practitioner conditions and preserves CUDA-level nondeterminism from floating-point non-associativity as a measurable variance source \\cite{messina2026background, thinkingmachines2025batch}. Few-shot prompting follows standard protocols: 5-shot for MMLU and ARC-Challenge, 8-shot for GSM8K, and 10-shot for HellaSwag.",
          "+Inference runs on vLLM~0.9.2 with default settings and without batch-invariant kernels. Few-shot prompting follows standard protocols: 5-shot for MMLU and ARC-Challenge, 4-shot for MATH, 8-shot for GSM8K, and 10-shot for HellaSwag.",

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "524e5c51-6099-4fa1-b8b8-857701d0a434",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "e18068a2-89ff-48e1-97c4-b5364069a907",
  "type": "user",
  "uuid": "9ea577bb-6e5d-45ec-8051-c6a391c29549",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519/projects/nlp_variance_gtheory/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
    "oldString": "Our work extends these approaches by crossing seven facets across four benchmarks",
    "newString": "Our work extends these approaches by crossing seven facets across five benchmarks",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related}\n\n\\paragraph{Variance Decomposition in ML/NLP Evaluation.}\nFactorial decomposition of evaluation variance has roots in the ML reproducibility literature. \\citet{bouthillier2021accounting} introduced an ANOVA-based template that decomposes benchmark variance across implementation details, hyperparameters, and data splits, establishing that design choices account for a substantial share of reproducibility failures. Several subsequent studies applied similar decomposition logic to specific facets of model evaluation \\citep{dodge2020finetuning, henderson2018deep, magnusson2023reproducibility}. \\citet{mesting2026hidden} brought Generalizability theory into LLM evaluation, decomposing variance across five methodology facets on a single benchmark; judge identity dominated at 43.8\\%, with prompt$\\times$judge interaction at 12.4\\%. However, this study used a single benchmark, included no infrastructure facets such as numerical precision, and fixed the judge as an LLM evaluator rather than isolating other sources. \\citet{kunievsky2025intent} proposed an alternative decomposition that separates intent uncertainty, articulation variance, and model uncertainty, but this framework does not cross infrastructure and methodology facets in a factorial design. Our work extends these approaches by crossing seven facets across four benchmarks, enabling direct comparison of variance structures across evaluation formats.\n\n\\paragraph{Infrastructure and Prompt Variance.}\nA parallel line of work has examined individual variance sources in isolation. On the infrastructure side, \\citet{yuan2025layercast} documented up to 9\\% accuracy variation from numerical precision and GPU type but provided no factorial attribution. \\citet{messina2026background} formalized the concept of background temperature from floating-point non-associativity in GPU computation, and \\citet{thinkingmachines2025batch} proposed batch-invariant kernels to mitigate CUDA nondeterminism. On the methodology side, \\citet{sclar2024formatting} found up to 76\\% accuracy swings from prompt formatting changes on fixed hardware, and BrittleBench \\citep{brittlebench2026} reported 50\\% variance from prompt perturbation. \\citet{pezeshkpour2024ordering} showed that answer ordering in multiple-choice benchmarks introduces systematic bias, while \\citet{bui2025seeds} and \\citet{biderman2024lessons} documented sensitivity to random seeds and training configurations. Each of these studies isolates one factor while holding others fixed, which precludes estimating interactions between sources or comparing their relative magnitudes within a unified design.\n\n\\paragraph{IRT and Efficient Benchmarking.}\nItem Response Theory (IRT) offers a complementary perspective on evaluation design by modeling item difficulty and discrimination parameters. \\citet{lalor2019irt} introduced IRT-based item analysis for NLP benchmarks, and \\citet{rodriguez2021leaderboards} showed that IRT reveals hidden properties of evaluation items that raw accuracy scores obscure. Building on this foundation, \\citet{polo2024tinybenchmarks} developed tinyBenchmarks, using IRT-based subsampling to reduce evaluation cost while preserving ranking accuracy, and \\citet{zhou2026irt} recommended a 116-item subset via PSN-IRT for efficient MMLU evaluation. \\citet{perlitz2023efficient} explored related strategies for benchmark compression. These approaches optimize item selection for latent trait estimation but do not model variance from prompt, seed, or ordering facets. IRT-selected items evaluated under a single methodological condition can therefore systematically underestimate the number of items required for reliable measurement across diverse evaluation settings.\n\n\\paragraph{Binary Data and Psychometrics Foundations.}\nOur application of G-theory to binary correctness scores draws on established psychometric methodology. \\citet{shavelson1991generalizability} and \\citet{brennan2001generalizability} provide the canonical framework for multi-facet reliability analysis. \\citet{mushquash2006gtheory} extended G-theory to binary data, and \\citet{jiang2018bayesian} developed logistic-link generalized G-theory for binary outcomes. \\citet{tuerlinckx2006glmm} established the connection between generalized linear mixed models and G-theory variance components. These methods ground our use of Henderson Method~I for binary correctness scores (\\S\\ref{sec:binary}).\n\n\\paragraph{Positioning.}\nRelative to \\citet{mesting2026hidden}, we fix the judge (generation-based scoring) to isolate the remaining facets; their judge-dominated variance (43.8\\%) would otherwise mask all other sources. Our multi-benchmark design reveals format-conditional structure that single-benchmark studies cannot detect. Relative to IRT approaches \\citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks, zhou2026irt}, G-theory and IRT optimize orthogonal dimensions of evaluation design: IRT selects informative items, while G-theory determines the conditions needed for a target reliability. Our D-study quantifies this complementarity: multi-facet designs require 10.0$\\times$ fewer items than single-condition protocols for equivalent reliability. Relative to isolated-factor studies \\citep{yuan2025layercast, sclar2024formatting, brittlebench2026}, our design crosses infrastructure and methodology facets within a single factorial framework, enabling interaction estimation that per-factor studies cannot provide.",

Several subsequent studies applied similar decomposition logic to specific facets of model evaluation \\citep{dodge2020finetuning, henderson2018deep, magnusson2023reproducibility}. \\citet{mesting2026hidden} brought Generalizability theory into LLM evaluation, decomposing variance across five methodology facets on a single benchmark; judge identity dominated at 43.8\\%, with prompt$\\times$judge interaction at 12.4\\%. However, this study used a single benchmark, included no infrastructure facets such as numerical precision, and fixed the judge as an LLM evaluator rather than isolating other sources. \\citet{kunievsky2025intent} proposed an alternative decomposition that separates intent uncertainty, articulation variance, and model uncertainty, but this framework does not cross infrastructure and methodology facets in a factorial design. ```text
1	\section{Limitations}
2	\label{sec:limitations}
3	
4	Our findings are bounded by three scope constraints. First, the model sample comprises four open-weight 7--9B parameter models from four architecture families; the results do not generalize to API-based models, larger models, or fine-tuned variants. Second, all experiments run on single-GPU inference (NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000); multi-GPU tensor parallelism introduces additional numerical non-associativity that our design does not capture. Third, the benchmark sample covers three multiple-choice and one free-form task; other evaluation formats such as code generation, long-form text production, and multi-turn dialogue may exhibit different variance structures.
```

</details>

          " To test whether the variance structure observed on MMLU generalizes across evaluation formats, exp-002 extends the design to five benchmarks: MMLU, ARC-Challenge, and HellaSwag (multiple-choice), and GSM8K and MATH (free-form generation). The design crosses 8 models $\\times$ BF16 $\\times$ 2 temperatures (0.0, 0.7) $\\times$ 6 prompts $\\times$ 6 seeds $\\times$ 4 orderings $\\times$ 200 items per benchmark, totaling 2,304,000 records. This reduced crossing fixes precision at BF16 and uses two temperature levels to concentrate statistical power on format-conditional differences. The ordering facet operates only on MMLU, where it selects among four permutations of the few-shot demonstration examples. For all other benchmarks, the prompt construction does not use the ordering parameter; ordering is structurally inert ($\\sigma^2_{\\text{ordering}} = 0$ by construction) and is retained solely for design balance. The two temperature levels bracket the most common evaluation configurations: deterministic ($\\tau = 0.0$) and a moderate stochastic setting ($\\tau = 0.7$) representative of practical deployment.",
          " ",

          " ",
          " We conduct six robustness analyses to verify that the reported variance structures are not artifacts of specific analytical choices. \\textbf{Item resampling} (exp-001b): three independent 200-item samples drawn from the full MMLU pool and evaluated under a BF16-only 5-facet design yield item difficulty variance shares of 73.2\\%, 72.6\\%, and 69.0\\% (CV = 3.1\\%) and $G_\\text{item}$ values with CV = 0.5\\%. The higher item share relative to the 6-facet baseline (58.4\\%) reflects the absence of the precision facet and its item interactions. Five additional robustness analyses (estimator comparison, CI method, facet specification, leave-one-model-out, and null calibration) are reported in Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness}; all confirm the stability of the reported variance structure.",

We now test whether the item-dominated variance structure generalizes across evaluation formats by extending to four benchmarks spanning two response formats.\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519/projects/nlp_variance_gtheory/docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex:55:\\paragraph{Design.} The expanded design (exp-002) crosses 4 models $\\times$ 4 benchmarks $\\times$ BF16 $\\times$ 2 temperatures (0.0, 0.7) $\\times$ 6 prompts $\\times$ 6 seeds $\\times$ 4 orderings $\\times$ 200 items per benchmark, totaling 921,600 records. The benchmarks include three multiple-choice tasks (MMLU, ARC-Challenge, HellaSwag) and one free-form generation task (GSM8K). For free-form tasks, the ordering facet permutes few-shot exemplar sequences rather than answer options.\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519/projects/nlp_variance_gtheory/docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex:57:% [PLACEHOLDER: expand to 8 models × 5 benchmarks (+ MATH) when data ready]\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519/projects/nlp_variance_gtheory/docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex:65:\\caption{Format-conditional variance structure across four benchmarks (exp-002, 921,600 records).
